Necessarily a state of slacluiess supervened, which resulted in the chapel and its various institutions suffering. Still everything was now improving in a most satisfactory manner. In oonolusion, Mr. Young said there was- one question he would like to bring before them, and that was the abominable noise they had to submit to in Cairo-street during the service. They were aware that near many places of worship in Warrington the roads were paved with wood much to the benefit and comfort of the congregation. (Hear,hear.) He hoped the com- mittee would see its way clear to approach the Town Council with a view to having Cairo-street paved with wood. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. F. W. MONKS, seconded by Mr. KNOWLES it was decided to use the Essex Hall Hymnal at the Sunday services, and the committee was authorized to purchase the requisite number. Mr.PEMßEßTON,superintendent of the Sunday school reported that the number of scholars on. the books up to the 27th September last, was 119 girls and 131 boys, making a total of 250 scholars. Although the school suffered in the early part of the year when the pastorate was vacant, it was now in a flourishing state. The debating society formed last year,had been attendedwith success and had proved a means of bringing members of the congregation into closer touch with the school. In Mr. Cook they had a very good minister,who entered very actively into the work of the school and had infused new life into the various organizations. He had now passed the stage of a new minister and was looked upon as an old friend by most of the members of the church. (Applause.) The REV. C. HARVEY COOK said he wished first of all to tender his sincere thanks and appreciation to all the workers who carried forward so earnestly, and with such deep interest, the various organizations of the church. He also thanked the congregation for its larger attendance at both the morning and evening services. Hedid not look upon that as a tribute to the power of his preaching orhis way of conducting the services, but he took it that the congregations attended because they found the services helpful,giving them the peace of God which fell like a benediction, as one sometimes felt the peace steal into one's heart, on wandering by the calm sea shore. They had been four months together; they had had four months of each other, four months he might thiy on behalf of himself, and his wife and children, of delight, fonr months of mutual discovery and four months which he fervently hoped might be a presage of all the future. Without fulsomeness or adulation he oould say that the members of that church did attend public worship. He wished, however, that a few more could find it convenient to come on Sunday mornings. In conclusion he said the superintendent's hands would be'strengthened considerably in the work of the school if two additional teachers could be obtained. Mr. F. MONKS addressed the gathering. (Cw)yright.)

"Look, Margit! " exclaimed Erica, her merry mood contrasting strangely with my own 'sober one. "Look what Mr. Thorne has given me! Did you ever look upon such lovely flowers? I saw them almost before I saw him, and he knows that he could not have brought me a more pleasing gift than he has done, for I couldn't wait until he offered it to me before I seized my treasures. I shall put the best of them in my herbarium, and there I shall always be able to look at them." "Mr. Thorne is very kind, I am sure," I said coldly, offering him a limp hand to shake. "How do you do, Mr. Thorne? Allow me to congratu- l-te you upon the success of your gardener's skill. Ally poor efforts can never hope to produce any such blooms as you have at command." Only fancy me trying to ape the stiff, artificial 'snguage of a woman of the world! Our visitor might well look astonished at seeing me assume a role for which I was so radically unfitted. But the momentary glance which I levelled at his face revealed something else to me. For the first time since I had known him, I saw Mr. Thorne's face disfigured by anger; but I could hardly, after the recent bitter lesson I had had, be vain enough to suppose that my coldness was responsible for his annoyance, and, in any case, Erica's smiles would soon restore his good humour. Indeed, I soon began to think that he could not have been very angry after all, and the winsome couple were so merry during the rest of the evening that not a shadow of doubt remained.

How happy they looked! And how handsome he was! Handsome enough even for my beautiful sister, whom I at last was compelled to regard as a woman, not as the child she had hitherto seemed to be.

Even Aunt Elspeth noticed how devoted they were to each other, and she bent over to whisper to me how she was that dear Erica was likely to have such a suitable husband. After this the whole world became grey for me, and the &apses of gold have been few and far between until lately. But time passed swiftly for 7.lrica, and there came a day when all the village was gay with the news of her forthcoming marriage with Mr. Thorne. Very much to the surprise of everybody, it was announced that the yomag couple would live akroad. But if anyone imagined that this would be a trouble to me they were mistaken. All I craved for was to get the wedding over, and to lose sight of both my loved ones until my burstins, heart had had time to overcome the terrible pain that oppressed it. Erica was wild with delight at the prospect of seeing the world, and of meeting with the homage which she confidently expected to receive from society. At first it was balm to me to listen to her joyous rhapsodies. But by-and-bye I became aware of a new source of anxiety and wonderment. Erica lost none of her delight in the constant discussions concerning the future which were per- petually to the fore. But it was impossible to ignore the fact that the ma.i was gradually becoin- ing secondary in importance to the gifts he 'could bestow upon her. Her fickle temperament had re- asserted itself, and it became apparent that wealth and position would suffice for her, even though unaccompanied by the love of which she had boasted so exultantly only a few months ago. That which she had then spoken of as nothing short of worship was, in point of fact, merely the glow, of gratified vanity at the idea of having won a lover who was coveted by nearly all the girls she knew. Then it was that I began to tremble for Hector's happiness, and I prayed that he might never be iisillusioned. Yet sometimes I feared that he guessed the truth already, for his spirits were less buoyant than before the engagement, and I Are shan once caught him looking at me with a 2eproachful sadness in his eyes which puzzled me exceedingly.

The wellare of these two was my principal object in life, and the bare suspicion that this object might fail to be fulfilled was enough to make my heart ache worse than ever it had done before.

But why should Hector look at me so reproach- fully? He surely could not imagine that I was to blame for any falling off there might be in the sincerity of Erica' g dffection fore her affianced husband! Of course, he had no idea of my own great love for 'him, and he was never likely to blow that I had placed his and Erica's happiness on a much higher plane of regard than my own. But, even though he was ignorant on these points, it was inconceivable that he should have found a grievance of any sort against me, and I puzzled vainly over the problem of trying to discover the origin of his displeasure with me. Nor did time seem to mend matters, for the nearer the wedding-day approached the more gloomy and reserved did Hector become with me, though, to all appearances, he was just the same bright, sociable being,with everybody else that he had always been, and his lovely bride could not complain of any lack of lavish attention on his tart.

[THE END. 1

--he wedding preparations' went on apace, and ~t least one individual was satisfied with them, for neither expense nor trouble was spared to make it the prettiest ceremony that had ever taken place in our village, and the grand doings we were having were the talk of the surrounding neigh- bourhood.

Burin.. the whole of the preparations I was kept too busily employed to have time to allow my own feelings to assert themselves very strongly. But sometimes I felt as if my fortitude could not endure much longer; and I prayed constantly for strength from above, to enable me to pass through the ordeal of seeing the love which I had once believed to be mine lavished upon a comparative child, who was already shewing, in many little ways, that she did not know how to value the inestimable treasure she had won so easily.

At last the wedding was over, and the bride and bridegroom were almost ready to leave us. Erica went upstairs to chancre her bridal robe for a travelling dress, and I was about to follow her when Hector stopped me with a peremptory gesture that compelled instant obedience. "I must have a few words with you in private, Margit," he said. "This may be the last oppor- tunity I shall have, and what I have to say to you will not bear keeping." Now that the moment of parting was so near I could scarcely speak for emotion, and I silently led the way to my own little summer-house in the f:arden, where we were not likely to be interrupted. "Marcrit," said Hector moodily, and the pas- offilonate "love which shone from his eyes made me shrink away from him, in startled fear of what was coming, "Your plans have been beautifully laid, and they have succeeded perfectly. You lured me on to love you, and then met me with chilling coldness as soon as vulgar gossip coupled my name with that of your sister. Good God, woman! Is your heart made of stone, that you could thus play with a man's most sacred emotions? Was it nothing to you that your tardily-shewn dislike was fain to blast my life for ever? So long as your doll of a sister realised her ambition, you were satisfied, and you left no means unused that could make it seem dishonourable for me to reject the love that was thrown at me, but which I did not covet. Your plans have been successful. I have married Erica, for it was all one to me what became of me, so long as I could not win your love. Even though scorned by you, I have had the melan- choly satisfaction of serving you, since it was solely to gratify your wishes that I married the one being for whose happiness you seem to feel any solicitude. But take care how you use your fatal beauty to break another man's heart as you have broken mine!"

As Hector thus pulled the scales from my eyes and sheived me the utter uselessness of the sacrifice I had made for Erica's sake, the light seemed to die out of the sky, and I felt as if my soul itself were leaving me. Some of the agony I endured ..at have shewn itself on my face, for the expres- L:re on that of Hector's suddenly changed, and he 4;ttgered forward, almost prostrated by the so• ivjohtnin revelation which had. come to,him also. "God help us both!" he moaned. "Too late! Too late!"

Then, imprinting one passionate kiss upon my anguished face, he left me, with only one hope in life—that Erica might never learn the terrible truth.

Twelve months passed by, and during all this tune I never received one letter from Hector. Erica, on the contrary, was always sending me a chatty bit of news of some sort. Her whole life seemed to be spent in the pursuit of pleasure, and it must be confessed that in her case the pursuit was not in vain. Every letter that reached me was full of worldly, frivolous gossip, and there was never the slightest sign that Erica had. any cause to regret her marriage. • Her husband was wealthy, and indulgent to a fault. Eery whim of hers was' gratified without demur, and the couple flitted from one Continental resort to another, their movements being apparently guided by no stronger motives than those which actuhte a 'butterfly as it flits from flower to flower. "And the funny part of it is." wrote Erica, "that -Hector seems Vo be far too dull, and gloomy to enjoy any of the gaieties that come in our direc- tion, though he is never bad-tempered, and he lets me have my own way in everything; nor does a single wish of mine remain ungratified. It is

lucky for me, however, that lam of a cheerful i temperament, and that I know how to amuse myself. Otherwise I should most likely be very dull indeed, for Hector is too prosy for words. Give him a book and a pipe, and he will amuse himself with them for a whole evening without troubling himself about anything further. I had no idea his tastes were so quiet, and it is all the nicer of him to be so indulgent to me, for it must be a bit of a bore to him to be kept always on the move."

The few deductions which I was able to make from these letters helped to reconcile me partially to the unfortunate train of circumstances which had resulted in the mistake which Hector and I had both made.

Whatever he anC ingot suffer, 11 was evident that Erica had everything she desired, and that, in this respect at least, the sacrifice I had made was certainly not a vain one. It did not matter to her that Hector did not love her for love, as I under- stood it, was by no means necessary for her happi- ness.

believe that a great many men, when brought face to face with such a revelation as had come to him on his 'wedding morniAg, would have done something desperate. But he was taking things so quietly as to make me hope that his disappointment was very much less keen than my own. As for me, I had all my life schooled myself to subvert my own interests to those of others, and the consciousness of having succeeded in doing so was always a source of subdued happiness in itself. When several months had rolled by, I ceased to expect any communications from Hector himself; and it was with a flutter of alarm that I opened a telegram which hailed from Interlaken, whence Erica's last letter had been dated.

"What can be the matter ? " I asked myself. "Mica promised me a letter by this mail, in which I was to hear all about an Alpine excursion in which she and Hector were going to take part. Surely there cannot have been an accident!" This foreboding struck me as soon as I opened the.telegram, and the few words it contained were more than sufficient to c'onfirm my fears. "There has been an accident," I read, "and we both need you at once. Start immediately." The telegram was signed "Thorne," but there was nothing to indicate whether it came from Erica or from Hector, and it was impossible for me to tell which of the two had been injured in the accident.' Yet it was evident that they were both still alive, and I could only pray that such injuries as they had received would not prove very serious, after all.

Soon after the departure of my sister and her husband for the Continent I had received a cheque- book from the latter, with instructions to draw at any time upon his banker, to the extent of at least two hundred pounds per annum. But I had not cared to avail myself of this liberality, and I had as yet not drawn a penny from the bank. No,w, however, there was no reason why I should allow my pride to prevent me from using the funds at my disposal, for I was too poor to undertake a Conti- nental journey at my own expense, and it was quite certain that, even if this, had not been the case, Hector would naturally have expected to be allowed to spare my purse as much as possible under the mrcumsttmces.

I made arrairgeraents for the comfort of Aunt Elspeth during my absence, but how I reached Interlaken I can hardly remember, though I shall never forget the terrible shock I received when Hector himself came to meet me at the station. I knew then, before I was told in so many words, that I was too late, and that my beautiful, wayward 3ister was one of the victims who are sacrificed every year to the love- of adventure. The full details of that dreadful time are too painful for me to dwell upon, and I am not sure that Hector's troubles were not enhanced, instead af being diminished, by my arrival on the scene, for the Shock of Erica's death had such an effect upon me that I was worse than useless as a 3onsoler.

Yet through the chastened vista of years lam %lways able to feel glad that my darling was never permitted to know that she was the innocent means af bringing trouble to Hector and me. And lam quite sure that, if her spirit is permitted to revisit is, she will approve of the quiet happiness which aas come to us at last, after years of waiting; for ;here are no misunderstandings now betweei . my sear husband and me.

A. PAGE FROM A WOMAN'S LIFE.

BY MRS. GEORGE CORBETT, Author of "The Adventures of an Ugly Girl," "The Star of Yukon," "A Mere M asquerader, " &e.

"Margit, I'm the very happiest girl that ever lived! "

I looked up in pleased interest as these words were addressed to me, and my gaze dwelt lovingly on the sparkling face and dainty little figure of the speaker. Erica had always seemed to me to be the fairest and sweetest picture that Nature could produce, and ever since our dying mother had com- mended her to my special care I had guarded her against the faintest I reath of trouble or anxiety. We two girls were all in all to each other, I and there were those among our friends who predicted that my indulgent treatment of my sister, who was my junior by six ,vears, would make her spoil* and selfish. But laughed at their pessimistic prognostications, and took ever-increasing delight in the sunny, joyous temperament of the being whom I had never permitted to feel the want of a mother's love.

Of late, however, a new element had entered my life, an element of such delightful, such enchant- ing possibilities that I held my very breath in prospective rapture as I tried to picture what a woman's life must be like when spent in the con- stant companionship of Hector Thorne. I was sitting in the orchard, my eyes dewy with blissful reverie, and my mind full of gratitude to Heaven for giving me the love that my heart had craved for, when Erica burst in upon me with the words that proclaimed a happiness as great as my own.

"Why, my darling," I said, "are you any happier dear, eor than usual?" su:31lll?y old sobersides! Of course I am! I have always been ihappy, but now—oh, Margit! Can't you guess ? " "No, I don't think I can, unless— Tell me, is there a lover in the case ? "

"To be sure there is! Hasn't there always been a lover in the case for at least three years now— ever since I was sixteen, in fact ? " "Yes, but a real lover—one whom you can love in return, in all seriousness ? " Somehow, though there was so little difference in our ages, I had always regarded Erica as a com- parative child, and had never deemed her numerous flirtations worth serious consideration. So I was certainly not prepared for the shock which pre- sently came to me.

"Aargi—t,-"--s-a-4 Erica, suddenly dropping her voice to a whisper, am almost frightened at the feeling that now possesses me, for it seems to me that love is too tame a word for it. It is wOrship. It is adoration!"

I looked in Erica's face in startled surprise, and hoped fervently that her love had not been mis- bestowed. But not the faintest suspicion of the terrible truth had as yet crossed my mind. "And he ?" I asked anxiously. "Does he love you? And is he worthy of your love ? " For answer she laughed merrily, for the doubt expressed in my questions seemed to her to be amusingly out of place. "Comie, dear," I urged. "Why don't you answer me? "

"Well," she replied, "to question his worthiness seems too absurd, for he is an acknowledged prince among such men as we have hitherto met. And of his love for me are there not proofs every day? Doesn't he fairly haunt the house I live in? Isn't he always paying me pretty compliments? Hasn't he bought me all sorts of dainty presents? And are not nearly all the girls in the village jealous because the handsomest and richest man in the neighbourhood cares for no society but mine ? " Each one of this string of questions dealt a death-blow at the happiness I had believed to be within my grasp. Each one increased the dread of coming misery which caused my limbs to shake under me, and my very heart seemed to stand still as I gasped out: "Child, don't I torture me so! Whom do you mean ? "

"Why, Whom should I mean but Hector Thorne ? " was the counter-query, given in the hurt tone of a child that takes naught but its own feelings into consideration. "You surely cannot have any objections to urge against him ? ' Objections! Great Heaven, what objections could I urge against the man with whom I had hoped to spend the rest of my life? But what a mad, vain fool I had been, to dream that I, whom no man had ever before sought in love, could have been the one to inspire that sentiment in the breast of Hector Thorne, the most perfect man I had ever met in my life! Happily, I had not betrayed myself to him—at least I hoped not—and Erica must never, never know that I had coveted the happiness which was destined for her. _ _

Ey a supreme effort I recovered the self-command of which despairing emotion had robbed me for a second, and I answered Erica's challenge so quietly as to surprise even myself. "He is, I believe, all that a,woman could wish for in a husband, and you know that lam never likely to object to anything that is necessary to your happiness. But—but—l never thought of Mr. Thorne as your lover, dear. . He is so much older than you!" • "He is only thirty, and he doesn't look anything like as old as you do, although you are younger than he is. But you are quite old-maidish already, and perhaps that accounts for it." "Yes, perhaps that accounts for it, I echoed, with such a dull, numbing pain at my heart as I had never felt before. "And you are quite sure that he loves you ?" "Of course I am! For what else but love of me could he come here so often? "

Ah! what else indeed! Who, or what, was I that I should have imagined it possible for such a man as Hector Thorne to be in love with me? Erica had put the case on its proper footing when with unconsciously cruel frankness she ignored the remotest likelihood of any attraction at Inglenook beyond herself. Poor old Great-aunt Elspeth, who came to live with us before mother died, spent the major part of her time in bed, leaving all the responsibilities of housekeeping to me, and, as Erica demonstrated to me, I was braver and quieter than is usual with women who have only numbered twenty-five summers. No doubt the heavy cares with which I was saddled when but early in my teens had some- thing to do with this premature seriousness of mine. Still, the fact remained that girls of my own age had always seemed to look upon me as predestined to old-maidism, and until lately I had never had reason to suppose that any man had thought of me except'as the staid elder sister of th. pride of the village. And now, God help me! Was all my happines, to be shattered at one blow? Had I really been deceiving myself when I imagined that Hector Thorne, who had only known us three months, had reversed the usual order of things, and that he had merely rega-rded Erica as the lovely, childlike sister of Margit Braham ? , Surely, my vanity could not lead me so far astray as that! And yet, alas! when I came to analyse the situation with a clearer vision, did I not realise that he was always merriest when Erica was by, and that she was invariably the recipient of the many little presents he had brought to Inglenook? Poor blind fool that I had been, not to see it all sooner! But it was not yet too late to save my pride and self-respect. Not a soul should ever know that the one love of my life had been wasted upon a man whose warmest sentiment for me was pity at my prospective old-maidish loneliness. So far as my sister was concerned, my duty was clear. Whatever befell me, she must always be happy. So, seeing how she loved this man, I would do all in my power to bring about a realisation of the happiness she coveted. That evening, Hector paid us his usual visit, and for the first time since I had known him I felt afraid to look him fairly in the face. Indeed, I would have taken refuge in my own room, if I had had time to do so. But Erica, having seen him coming, had hastened smilingly to the door '— meet him, and I had no chance to escape him without making my avoidance of him, more pointc,— and noticeable than I cared to allow to be the case.

"Erica is bringhig him straight here," I said to myself, feeling the blush‘of anticipated humiliation rise to my cheeks as I heard his well-known foot- step approaching the .little parlour which over- looked our beautiful garden. "There is nothing for it but to face out the meeting, and try to dis- abuse his mind of any suspicion .that I, staid, old- maidish Margit Braham, have allowed my vanity to cheat me so lunch. He shall certainly not go home this evening under a wrong impression, for, if necessary, I can make myself positively disagree- Able to him."

Having thus mapped out my future treatment cf Erica's rover, I set about carrying out my plan of campaign with such promptitude that I was able to greet Mr. Thorne with quite commendable coldness.

SOLDIER'S NOVEL SUICIDE.

Mail advices from Tonkin bring an account of a curi- ous case of suicide. A soldier in the French Colonial Regiment tied a rope to the last wagon on a goods train, made a running noose in the end, and put his head in the noose when the train started. The first stopping place was ten miles distant, when the remains of the man scarcely enough to identify him, were found at the end of the rope.

PREFERRED DEATH TO PENAL SERVITUDE. An extraordinary incident was witnessed at Glasgow Circuit Court on Tuesday, when a Russian-Pole named George Totnassewich pleaded guilty to stabbing with a long-bladed shoemaker's knife, a girl and two men,all Poles, in a house on the south side of Glasgow. Accused, it was explained was in love with the girl,who had repeatedly refused to marry him. On the night in question, mad with drink, he stabbed the girl in a fit of jealousy, afterwards wounding two men who interfered. The lives of the girl and men were despaired of for several days. Lord ,INloncr.teff imposed sentence of twelve years, when prisoner said he preferred to die.

A WORD ON RHEUMATISM. How Bile Beans Cure It.

First, what is it? It begins with a dull aching feeling in an arm, a leg, or in the back. When you rest and then move suddenly it hurts terribly. Sitting in a dry, warm room, you perhaps don't feel it. Go into a damp or cold place—back it comes. To-day is fine—you're free. To-morrow wet—it grips you again. Then the limb gets stiff. If it's rheumatism in the hand, the fingers hurt when straightened. If it's in the arms or legs, you feel afraid to move them. If you don't move them, they get more stiff. Sometimes so stiff that they won't bend. That's why so many walk with sticks! One day you feel it in one place another day in another. It's an acid in the blood. That's how it travels from limb to limb and place to place. Bile Beans purify the blood from this acid. That's how they drive rheumatisra out. Mr. Michael Greville, proprietor of the Farmer's Hotel, South Esplanade, Guernsey, says': "Some years ago I caught a chill,which brought on rheumatism The pains were very severe, and while they continued I was unable to attend to business or do work of any kind. The achin,g was all over my body, and I was so racked with pain that any movement, painful at all times, was agonising while the attacks were upon me. Rheumatism reduced my whole system, and other ail- ments crept in. On a friend's advice I tried Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. They quickly improved me, and by degrees cured me. It is now two years sinoe I last felt a trace of rheumatism, so the cures which Bile Beans effect are permanent." Bile Beans also cure liver complaints, indigestion, constipation, piles, all female ailments, headache,debil- ity, and rheumatism. TWO MEN BURIED ALIVE. INSUFFICIENT TIMBERING AND INADEQUATE SUPERVISION. The inquest on John William Platt, married, aged twenty-four, of 76, Hardshaw-street,and Thomas Grundy, single, aged twenty-three, of Raiaford-road, St. Helens, occupied a very con derable time on Monday at St. Helens Town Hall. v. H. L. Riley represented Platt's relatives, and Mr. E. Pence the colliery company. The men were daywagemen working in the Ravenhead Pit of the St. Helens Collieries for H. Critehley, con- tractor. On Thttrsday night they were buried under a tremendous fall of roof and killed.— Thomas Halliwell, shot-lighter, said he visited the men once, by direction of the under-manager, at a quarter to six. He believed the men had been visited at 3 p.m. Platt was the leading hand. He found the roof knocked safe. There was plenty of timber thirty yards from the men. The men had roofed down nearly two yards, and there was no timber for half a yard between the old part and the new, so that they would be two yards under the roof where it was not timbered.—By Mr. Riley : He could not stop to see the men put up a bar becausehe had other work to do.—The Coroner asked if it was not witness's duty to stop and see it done.— Witness said he told the men to do it.—Mr. Peace said it was a matter of discretion.—H. Critchley, contrac- tor, said the men were both competent to roof down. He told them to keep the place well timbered.—Mr. Matthews, Government Inspector, said the fall was extensive, and was caused by two slips. He thought there was not sufficient timbering done.—The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," but gave the opinion that there was insufficient timbering and inade- quate supervison of the men.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The meeting in connection with this society on Sunday was presided over by Mr. George Colley, who spoke upon the gambling evil. He urged his hearers to shun betting, as they would smallpox. If they could not all become wealthy, he said, they could at least all become honourable.—Mr. A. Barbury, of Oldham. delivered an address, in which he drew some useful lessons from the captivity and ultimate delivery of the laraelites. He spoke upon the, value of national hope- fulness, and asked his hearers to play the part of true men. Mr. Pearce efficiently sang two solos entitled " 0 Blessed Redeemer" and "Through the Darkness."— Mr. B. Farrar (the hon. secretary) said the society would this month complete its tenth year of existence. The membership had reached 559 which was slightly lower than last year. Mr. Farrar proceeded to refer to the approaching eleventh anniversary and asked the mem- bers to make it a red letter day and to endeavour to make the society a greater sphere of usefulness.—The service was also taken part in by Mr. J. Simcock and Mr. W. Beswick.

The Rev. H. Ifinudeo and the Eastern Pea

"The Eastern Peril: A warning to the West" was the subject of the Rev. H. Dunnico's discourse in the Baptist Church, Golborne-street. on Sunday evening. The rev, gentleman, did not hesitate to say that unless the people of the West fulfilled their obligations to the East,rere long it would be too late, and they would reap a terrible recompense. Alluding to the war between Japan and Russia he said that if the latter won one result of victory would be that she would firmly establish herself in Manchuria and control the whole of China, controlling China, Russia would control all Asia and before we were aware of it the Russo-Eastern peril would be upon Europe. He (the preacher) hated war as much as auy one, but now that the war had broken out his sympathies as a Christian were with Japan and the rev, gentleman explained why, adding that though Russia was nominally Christian it would not bear for a moment eomparison with Japan. Some interesting information was given as to the strength of China, and Mr. Dunnieo said he had it on reliable authority that if that country wore organized as Japan was she could muster an army of not less that 80,000,000 men. Before sue* a force European armies would he swept away. In conclusion, the preacher said that to each nation God had given a mission and the mission of England was the evangelization of the Asiatic races. As a nation we had done nothing to morally uplift the people of China, but we had done much to demoralize and degrade them. Chins had given many valuable things tro us; now we had an unparalleled opportunity of repaying her. A selfish nation would perish just as surely as a selfish man and a nation that existed solely to gain wealth and man, would be destroyed ; but a nation founded on devotion to God and founded in obedience to Him could not be other than great. Let all narrow imperialism and greed of Em- pi," perish; give him a people high in principle and devoted to God, then whether we were great or small the name of England wevild never-perish and we should have our place in the final establishment of the Kingdom of God upon the earth.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews and the Bribery Case. The Yellow Labour Question.

Preaching at Latchford Baptist Church on Sunday morning, the Rev. J. F. Matthews said there were two matters of more than ordinary importance he would ask the congregation to think about. Locally a case had been before the Courts, and a burgess of the town had been fined for bribery. The gentleman concerned had taken no mital part in the non-municipal trading agitation, and had, he believed been himself the agent for exposing some of the rotten methods that had marked our municipal life. Yet. singular as it might seem, he himself had now been convicted of what Judge Bigham described as "Contemptible bribery." No doubt that, together with his probable disfranchisement, would be sufficiently hard punishment. He mentioned the case for the double purpose (a) of directing attention to the low morals of public life in Warring- ton. There was no doubt the case just alluded to was by no means exceptional, but what must be said of the quality of the ideals held by many who would seek to serve the Commonwealth? What must be said of the poverty of many who sought the suffrages of their fellow citizens? (b) what was even worse seemed to be in the fact that there were people willing to be bought for a glass of beer, ora bit of money, people so vulgar,and with such a low conception of public morality that they were willing to sell their votes, and then boat of having done so. If in the one case to bribe, was a contemptible thing,to be bribbed was just as des- picable. The action in the case of a Christian man was almost unpardonable, but in the case of an unthinking, callous body of vote's, was scarcely less so. Surely it was time that the public sentiment demanded a higher ethical ideal on the part of its would-be servants land recognized, when it was forthcoming, a generous, unstinted, and unselfish devotion to the highest inter- ests of municipal life. The second matter was of terrible importance. He referred to the last reaction- ary work of this reactionary Government, in sanctioning the importation of yellow labour i into South Africa. Early t! last century millions of money were spent by this country in free- ing the slaves of the West Indies, and now, at the bidding of gold-grabbers, land-grabber, and hypocriti- cal diamond lords, the reimposition, of fetters upon an alien race was to be attempted. Did anyone seriously believe that the proposed safeguards were any safe- guards at all 7 The men who were most concerned to bring in slavery once again were the very men who hated white labour because it was too dear, and because white men wanted to have a voice in the affairs of South Africa. What had the workers in this country to say to this policy of pros- pective slavery? And what would. Wa rrington, Widnes, and St. Helens have to say to its "ho voted with the Government? We wore told that we went to South Africa to secure industrial and political freedom for the white man, and now the white man was to have no chance at all. The lust for gold was the death of all conscience. It was well that the wickedness of the wicked should come to an end, and. who knew but this last act in an ugly tragedy might bring about the down- fall and ruin of the villains. All well-wishers of South Africa would at least hope that "freedom" wc uld and that the reign of the mine-owner and slave- driver would speedily be brought to naug,ht.

"Canada's Capabilities."

A lantern lecture on:the above subject was delivered in Wycliffe Memorial Building on Monday evening, under the auspices of the 1 oung People's Society, by Mr. W. Beecher Smith, secretary of the Manchester Y.M.C.A. The Rev. John Yonge presided. Mr. Smith recently paid a visit to Canada in connection with the Young Men's Emigration Advice Society, which he said was established not for the purpose of persuading young men to emigeate, but to give advice upon the following points to any, who, with the con- sent of their parents or guardians, had decided upon emigration: (1) The best means of finding employ- ment on the land. (2) The present pros- pects in the towns and cities of Canada for suitable energetic young men seeking commercial situations. (3) The best mode of transit. (4). The necessary outfit, cost of passage, &c. (5) To help with introductions to personal friends and Young Men's Christian Associations in Canada and elsewhere. (6) To give such practical help as shall surround, With good influences, and kind friends, young men carrying letters from this society. The lecturer stated that the society had already done much good work in this direction. The lecturer took his audience on an imaginary journey across Canada, describing all the interesting places en route, and pointing out the capabilities of certain districts.—Mr. Beecher Smith was heartily thauked forlis lecture, and in responding, acknowledged the assistance of the lanternist (Mr. Watson).

Mr. F. Monks presided and there were also present :—the Revs. Morley Stevenson, E. J. THE BOROUGH TREASURER'S SALARY.

The Finance Committee directed him to point out that in their opinion the Education Committee were better qualified for dealing with the proposed increase, inasmuch as they were acquainted with the work which the Borough Treasurer performed for them, and before the Council could deal with the matter they would have to ask for a report from the Education Committee. Under the circum- stances the Finance Committee thought that the Edu- cation Committee skould consider the matter and make a recommendation to the Council.

The Finance Committee also submitted the follow- ing recommendations :—The committee proceeded to consider the salary of the Director, and the r.emenera- tion to be paid to the Borough Treasurer and the Borough Surveyor for the services rendered by them to the committee, in accordance with the instructions of the Education Committee at their meeting on the 18th January, 1904.—1 n view of the fact that the Town Council had, in the interval, increased the salary of the Borough Surveyor by £lOO par annum, the committee did not deem it necessary to make any recommendation with regard to that officer.—After some discussion it was resolved that the increment of the Directot's salary on the Ist April, 1904, and annually thereafter, be £5O instead of £2s,until the maximum be reached.— That, in view of the great additional rcsponsibility placed upon the Borough Treasurer as ascounting officer of the Education Committee, the Council be requested to consider the desirability of increasing his salary. MR. A. J. JOLLEY'S EXPLANATION.

In rroving the adoption cf the minutes and estimates, Mr. A. J. JOLLEY said the resolution with reference to the Dirostor's salary represented the opirion of the committee—though not the unanimous opinion. There was a slight difference, laid a division was taken, but the difference of opinion was only whether the increase should have been greater than was now receirmenci3d. If the resolution were passed the Directcr's salary for Lhe year commencing April let would be £375 and annual inorements of £5O would ifollow. The maximum of £5OO would not be reached for three years.

The Most Nutritious and Economical.

In regard to the estimates, very great care had been exercised in their preparation. The first draft was pre- pared by the Director and the Borough Treasurer, and after that he (Mr. Jolley) and the Director spent several hours going through the figures again, and to some extent reyising them. Subsequently each sub- committee dealt with its own section, and finally the Finance Sub-committee went through them. That evening the completion of that process of evolution was in the hands of the committee, and he hoped they would express their approval of the estimates. There ware, of course, some elements of uncertainty, and the fact that they had not yet had a full year's work in any department—and in some departments only an irregular fraction of a year—made an accurate esti- mate difficult. The heaviest item of expenditure— salaries of teachers—was worked out in acecrdance with the scales and regulations which that committee as a whole had already approved. The principal stain on the receipts side—Government grants—would,unless some unforeseen change in the methods of assessing and paying them was made, probably work out pretty closely by the end of tire year. There was an element of uncertainty due to the fact that grants earned in one financial year were sometimes paid, or partly paid, in another. The salient fact was that the deficiency represented a rate of 101 d. as against 10d. last year. It was desirable to remember that of the 102ci. in the 2,22 d. represented the cost of duties de- volving on the Corporation prior to the Act of 1902 in connection with technical instruction and school attendance work, wnilsi; Bd. represented the expendi- ture due to the operation oZ the Education Act. With reference to the School Manage.nent Committee's estimates under Higher Education, he might say that the Secondary school had only been open for a part of the caerent year. Certain changes aimed at securing the highest attainalle measure of useihrinese from the Technical Institute, and drawing a larger number of youths and g:rls into the evening schools had been made this season as compared with last. The results had justified the action oi the committee, and it was proposed during the coming financial. year to continue the development on similar lines. That pointed to an increase of anneal expenditure of about £7OO, which sum, it was estimated, would be more than recouped by the inoresed grcnta. Under elementary edacadon an increase was made in the item " apparatus , and furniture " of aieout £5OO, an eatimcte ' which, he thought, was certainly not too large On the L1C0328 side; the item. "fees, £1,698," was what they estimated taey would have received during the current year under the existing arrangements. He would like it to be distinctly understood that any pro- posal to reduce fees, or to hand over an increased portion of them to the managers of fee-charging echools during the corning financial year, would increase the deficiency, and 83 involve an addition to ,the rates. Personally, he was strongly of opinion that the exist- ing errangements in respect of fees should continue in force for at leaet another year, bht it was, of course open to anyone holding a contraey ooitlion to propose a reduction of it, or the deletion of that item, mid a farther increase of the rates by Id. or 2d.. as the case might be. He hoped the committee would approve of the estimat3, which was based on the assumption that the existing systain would remain in operation for another year. Broadly speeking, the Octlr3S in the Finance Com- mittee's estimate showed tnat cleaning heating and Eghting cost about ss. per annum per 'awl.] of the aver- age attendance. The item for "repayment of principal and interest on loan to provide working balance "was in accordance with the sanction granted eto the local authority, and but for which last year's rate would have been 61d. higher than it was. There was nothing calling , for special mention in the Sites and Buildinks Com- mittee's estimates, except to say that the estimate for fair wear and tear was an exceedingly modest one. As would be seen from the summary, the total expenditure was estimated at £40,025 17s. 5d., and of that the teachers' salaries alone were by far the biggest item. The total receipts amounted fto £29,625, the Govern- ment grants, of course, being the main item. If they worked out all the figures they would find that of the total CO3G of education to Wan ington,about one-quarter fell on tfie rates, eleven-sixteenths on Imperial taxes, and one-sixteenth was made up .1 fees rind sundries. He moved the confirmation of the minutes and the estimates.

Alderman PIERPOUT secouded. THE DIRECTOR'S SALARv.

Councillor TINNION pointed out that in the School Management Committee's estimates £5O had been put down for instruction in drill. He thought that was not sufficient, as it did not at all adequately represent the amount spent. A wider aspect of the question which would have to be faced was that at present gymnastic instruction was being given to the children attending only five oftwenty-three schaels.TheGynanaKum Commit tea had drawn up a report,and itwould be brought up at some later date. At any rate, in the new provided schools, they might make provision for drill grounds. As Chairman of the Gymnasium Committee, hs was not willing to allow £5O to be put down, when the amount would be at least £225. He moved that £.225 be put down in place of. £5O. Alderman SMETHURST seconded. Mr. JoLLEv: I have no hesitation whatever in accept- ing that. Alderman PIERPOINT concurred, and said ,he would like to point out that every schoolmaster in Germany was able to put his school through theproparamornltof drill. The question of military drill, he considered, might be very properly taken up by the elementary school teachers themselves. It would be a pleasing rather than an onerous duty.

A VOICE: Jt is done already. Mr. UPSON took exception to a remark that the only egicient drill W2S given at the Gymnasium. It .was quit 6 true, perhaps, that they did not have exactly the same kind of drill. For one reason they had not the apparatus. - Councillor Ti:cmoN : Therefore. It is not egicient.

Mr. UPSON : It does not follow that the -gymnastic

system of drill is the best. Continuing, Mr. Upson said he believed all the teachers in the town—or nearly all —had undergone a course of drill. Some of them were sergeants in the volunteers ;others had taken courses of drill under sergeant-instructors at the Barracks ; and others had attended classes at the ,Gymnasium. They were compelled by the code to teach th 3 childrea drill on two occasions each week. They were inspected in the drill given, and he did not think the drill was com- plained of by the inspectors. To his mind, the drill at many schools was better than gymnastic drill. If that committee would provide then with apparatus they tcould make the drill quite as efficient as that given at ha Gymnasium.

Alderman BENNETr said Mr. Upson admitted there was a deficiency in regard to apparatus. The matter was of such v.tal importance to the welfare of the young people, that ecen if it involved a still further addition to the estimates, he did not think they ought to stint the schools in that respect. Not only should the utmost attention be given to ordinary drill, but the children should aiso be taught how to SiVtlll. Tihe Rev. Father WILSON said he could speak in erffire corrob&ration oP. what Mr. Upson had said, as the drill given by the teachers was entirely eacient and in his opinioa, more suitable to the children than gym- nastic drill.

Colonel BUCKT3N said he eutirely approved of the amendment, as he thought all the things which came under the head of education should be properly accounted, so that the town would know what education really cost. The increasing of the sum' as suggested by Councillor Tinnion, did not increese the cost on the rates, but was merely transferring the cost from one department to another. He agreed with Alderman Bennett as to children being taught how to swim and, if possible, to ride.

Mr. R. r. FAIRCLOUGH rernark:,d that at Church- straet school they had plenty of room, and the drill was, he thought, fairly efficient. Ho thought they might put down £lOO for drill instruction, and £lOO for proyaling apparatus for the schools, instead of £225 as one item.

Councillor TINNION hoped Mr. Fairclough was not under the impression that the whole cost of the drill for the children of Warrington would be £2OO. Tf they were to take all the schools it would reach £7OO or £BOO a year.

The 15zioscroa said he believed most of the schsols which required apparat,us had been supplied by the committee or the managers. Some schools did not require any. Taking it as a whole he thought tbey had got what was required. The amendment was then carried, Mr. May dissenting.

HOW TO BECOME A MILLIONAIRE.

There areas many recipes for the performance of this trick as there are for the making of cakes. "Practice all the virtues," says one. "Deny yourself," advises another; "fight against every instinct of your nature which costs either money or time; work and save ; aid when you are very old and about to die you shaLl have the gratification of being envied for your res." Another man who has amassed more than a million by trade and is still under fifty, says : " Never manu- facture anything; buy cheaply. sell dearly, and give no credit." A Ohioago millionaire attributes his success to his habit of regarding every man as a thief until he has proof of his honesty—a cynical perversion of- the older and more charitable plan of regarding every man as honest until he proves himself the reverse. Clearly,the wise ones who would teach us how to become million- aires are very numerous and no two of them tender the same advice.

All these teachers may be right, though it is more probable that all of them are wrong. But one thing is sure—no man ever yet became a millionaire as a result of following advice,however good that advice might be. Another thing equally certain is that no dyspeptic has ever been known to acquire a fortune; his headache, wearieess, and depression make it impossible. It would ssem, therefore, that a good first step towards success is investment in a bottle of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, and so insure freedom from indigestion. "For sixteen years I suffered from indigestion, at times so severely that I could not work." Thus writes Mr. Joseph IVlullens, of the Post Office, Tatsfield, Sarrey, on 16th October, 1903. Mr. Mullens continues : "Whatever I ate was sure to cause me great pain. I was much troubled with violent head- aches and dizziness. Asif this were not enough foe MO to endure, I suffered from frequent attacks of billowi- ness, which badly affected my sight. Some medicine proscribed for me by a doctor having failed to do me any good, I was induced to tev Mother Seigel's Syrup, and very soon found relief. The pain after eating, the headache, aad dizziness all left me. Soon I could enjoy my food as I had not done for yeirs, and a very few battles were sufficient to make me as well as ever I was in my life. Iswould not on any abcount be ,without some of the Syrup at band in case of need." Many people are pravented from acquiring wealth only by HI-health, while some who acquire it lose their health in its pursuit, and so derive no benefit from their success. The remedy is to act as Mr. Miallens did.

STOCKTON HEATH.

FREE TRADE AND PROTECTION—A public debate will take place on Monday evening in the Providence school- room the subject arising out of the above being "Would Mr. Chamberlain's proposals benefit the working class or otherwise ?"—Mr. McDermott will endeavour to prove that they will do so, and he is expected to be supported in this idea, by Messrs. H. E. Hughes. H. J. Westbrook and others, whilst Mr. E. Lloyd Jones will on the other hand endeavours to show the fallacy of such ideas and will be supported by Messrs J. Sherburn, E. England, J. Brown and others. The chair will be taken.at 7.30 by Mr. J. H. Clarke the chairman of the Parish Council.

WEDDING AT HILL CLIFFE.—A wedding took place on Thursday morning at Hill Cliffe Chapel, the.parties being Mr. Wilson Astbury, formerly of Warrington, but now of Bolton, and Miss Annie Arnold, the third daughter of the late Mr. William Arnold, master monu- mental mason of Stockton Heath.—The best man was Mr. B. S. Newcombe and the chief bridesmaid Miss Evelyn Arnold the youngest sister of the bride. The Rev. J. S. Hughes, who happens to be the brother-in- aw of the happy bride, officiated at the ceremony. EBENEZER WESLEYAN CHAPEL.—Theannual tea party and distribution of prizes in connection with the Sun- day school was held on • Thursday evening. About 70 eat down to tea, the tables being presided over by Mesdames Moss, Proud, Summerfleld,and Berry,assisted by the Misses E. Proud, A. and Strange. The Revs. G. H. Carnson and Joseph Howard. An entertainment was afterwards held, the Rev. J. Howard presiding. Mr. H. Brereton (secretary) presented his annual report which showed that the school was in a flourishing state. The Chairman delivered an address in which he made special reference to the excellent report,and spoke words of encouragement to the parents and scholars. Mr. S. B. Stringer (superintendent of the school) also addressed the gathering on Sunday school work, and Mr. A. Lamplugh gave a brief review of the history of Methodism in Warrington. Fify-one prizes were distributed by the Rev. Jos. Howard, and after- wards Mr. S. Moss alluded to the encouraging pros- pects of Methodism in Stockton Heatia.—Songs were very creditably rendered by Mrs. Hodgson, and Miss M. Evaes, and the choir sang "The Glory Land" with good effect.—Miss Elsie Miller presided at the organ. A vote of thanks were accorded to the Chairman and all who had assisted.

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

Lecture at Wycliffe Memorial Buildings.

Eleven passive resisters will, it is expected, be sum- moned before the Warrington magistrates next month. On Wednesday evening a well-attended meeting was held in Wycliffe Memorial Hall, when the Rev. J. S. Hughes delivered a lecture on" Passive. Resisters : Past and present." Mr. N. Bairn ford, president of the War- rington Citizens' League, presided, and in the course of a brief speech, said that passive resistance to-day needed no apology. It had come to stay until its work was done, and already there were signs of success. In spite of the letters from the Archbishops down to curates and assistant curates, from North, 'East, South and West, they were going along swimingly, and there was no danger of their being swamped. Already about 11,000 summonses had been issued, and six resisters had gone to prison. The REV. J. S. HUGHES then delivered the lecture which has been prepared by "The Crusader." It dealt with passive resistance, as displayed at various periods of our national history. It was pointed out, wrongly imagined in some quarters that such a method of pro- test was peculiar to the pursent day. As a matter of fact, the Christians of AMlostic times suffered by the spoiling of their goods and, indeed, rejoiced that they were counted worthy to suffer such a shame. A critic of the movement now in progress was recently roused to mirth on the occasion of the sale by auction of a kettle and alrabbit hutch. To an outsider, there might seem to be only a kettle, only a rabbit hutch. To the resister, and to all who understood the protest,there was an important and vital principle involved ; and the goods were but mere symbols of treasures of far higher value and worth. Behind the chattels of the resister there was the enraged conscience. John Hampden was doubtless ridiculed because he resisted the payment of a paltry twenty shillings as ship-money. George Fox objected to the taking of a mere oath because his con- science forbade him; and thousands of martyrs had suffered by flame and sword, preferring death to recantation, though it involved but the utterance of a single word. The lecturer then proceeded to briefly sketch the lives of men who might be called "passive resisters" of the past, commencing with the refusal of the barons, with Wycliffe and the Commons, to pay tribute demanded from England by Pope Urban the Fifth. The bold stand was decisive; and from that time no demand was ever again made by the Pope for tribute from the realm of England. Coming to the pre- sent day, it was stated that the Education Act of 1902 provided for the destruction of one of the ehief safe- guards of the nation's liberty; that was, the full repre- sentative control of publicly-provided funds. It undermined the primary right of man in society to the free use of his own conscience in the things which '- pertained to religion. Again, it felled at one stroke the strong'and well-constructed education ediflce,vvhieh had taken the State more than a quarter of a century to erect; but, worst of all, the Government undertook this prodigious work of reaction and ruin without any request from the nation, and simply because it had at its disposal the heavy war battalions enlisted at the last General Election. Under the new Act Nonconformists would have to pay for the maintenance of 11,000 denominational schools in which dogmatic teaching was given, which was directly contrary to th ir sincere and conscientious belief, and against which their very Nonconformity was a standing protest. In seven thousand parishes, Nonconformist parents would be compelled to send their children to, and contribute to the support of, a school where the children were taught to despise the religion of their parents. Sixteen thousand head teacherships would be absolutely closed to Noncon- formists by means of religious" tests," although all the teachers were now public servants of the State. This was a distinct bribe held dazzlingly before the eyes of young Nonconformists to forsake the faith of their fathers, and so gain much-coveted promotion. In eleven thousand schools Nonconformist teachers would not even be appointed to subordinate posts except by special favour.

The lecture was illustrated with lantern views. At the close the Rev. J. S. HUGHES said he had received a letter from the Rev. John Yonge expressing regret that owing to an engagement at Pemberton he could not be present. He wanted it to be known that he was in full sympathy with the movement. He objected very much to anyone entertaining the idea that he was opposed be prcssive resistance, and it was only the position in which he was placed that precluded him from taking:an active part in it. He (the speaker) was-sure their friends would be glad of that eapression, for'Mr.Yonge had ever been in the van-guard for religious liberty.

A WONDERFUL STORY.

Arany women will read with pleasure and profit the story of Miss L. Williams, Hill Cottage, Cwmyoy Lower, Abergavenny. Miss Williams certainly is in a position to advise her suffering sisters, and her experi- ence should be a guide to all who have similar troubles. "I had for a long time been troubled with Palpitation of the Heart, and T was also subject to Headache Dizziness, Parched dry Throat, bad taste in the mouth and furred tongue. A was greatly troubled with pains across my back, lots of memory, fainting fits, and I used to be so tired and languid that I could do nothing, and the least excitement set my heart palpitating violently. I took Dodd's Kidney Pills, and they so•ri put me right, aid though I have tried various medicines, these Pills were the only remedy that did me any good. They completely cured rue,and I can recommend them." There is really no leason why any woman should con- tinue to suffer, for Dodd's Kidney Pills will cure all such cases, and they are for sale wherever medicine is sold.

PERKETIL

BAND CONCERT.--The people of Penketh have come to expect something special in connection with the concerts promoted by the Penketh Tannery Prize Band, and it is pleasing to note that they are rarely dis- appointed. The gathering on Friday night last, despite the extremely wet weather, was a very large one, and testified to the popularity of the concerts of the Band Committee. The programme opened with a well exe- cutea selection by the band. "Recollectidns of Flotow," and this was followed by a song by Mr. F. Fieldhouse"Soldier's Song" which was well received. Mr. W. Rimmer of Southport, the well known bands- man, favoured the audience with two cornet solos 'Carnival de Venice," and "Click, Clack polka." He was accompanied by Miss Rimmer on the pianoforte, and his very artistic performance was greatly appreciated. Miss Arline Harrison, is possessed of a captivating manner,as well as a very clear and sweet voice, and was quickly on good terms with the audience. Encores were demanded for her two songs "Love's Ransom," and " Love the Pedlar." A novel feature in this neigh- bourhood was the performance of Mr. W. Whittaker, whose two whistling solos "Stars and Stripes," and "Under the Double Eagle," were very popular, and he received a well earned encore. Mr. F. Fieldhouse's other song " The Toreador," was rendered with vigour and effect, and another selection by the band" Aria Bolero," was a well executed effort. Special mention should be made of the quartette, of bandsmen, namely, Messrs. F. Glover, H. Glover, A. Bowyer, and H. Plumpton, who rendered the difficult piece" Ii Trove- tore,' in a very efficient manner. Mr. W. Higham was in charge of this item. No concert, in the opinion of Penketh audiences, is complete without a humourist, and the selection of Mr. Arthur Bearle, to fill this role, wasa good one, and he earned repeated encores for his very funny turns. Mr. W. Kendrick officiated as accom- panist throughout.

THE EXAMINER.

FEBRUARY 27th 1904.

- A message from St. METHODS OF Petersburg has in- BARBARISM. formed the world that the To Deum had been sung at the Winter Palace in gratitude for the "Great Russian victory " at Port Arthur. Oatside of Russia nobody believes in great Russian victories. Admiral ALEXEIEFF is clearly not in- toxicated with joy over his very modest achieve- ments. In the face of the triumphant telegrams which announced the destruction of four Japanese battleships and two transports, the admiral does not know ho has destroyed anything except the four old merchantmen with which the Japanese tried to block the entrance of the haibour. They were sent there in order that they might be sank, and if the adventure has failed it is because they were not sunk in the right place. That is the whole extent of the "great Russian victory." Admiral ALEXEIEFF says three of his cruisers were recalled from pursuing the enemy that they might clear away the floating wreckage, and yet the: Russian telegrams talk as if the Russian fleet had steamed out and the Japanese fleet had run away. The fact is that the Japanese torpedo boats withdrew at their leisure. Even now, it is possible that the wreckage which has to be cleared away may prove to be exactly what the Japanese intended to leave behind. Admiral ALEXEIEFF says they /also left some "floating mines," and as the Russian ships have a knack of blowing themselves up with their own mines, they may be equally successful with the others. It is plain that Admiral TOGO can do nearly all he pleases at Port Arthur. For the purposes of sea power it is practically useless to Russis, and the Japanese Admiral can sail away for some little job elsewhere without any fear that the battered ships in the harbour will be fit for anything serious in his absence. The Vladivostok squadron, after sinking a Japanese merchantman, prudently returned to the shelter of the shore batteries. Whatever the Russian Army may do not a single cargo of supplies will react them by sea The coarse of events does not give us a favourable impression of Russian capacity— naval, military, or civil. Could any thing be more annoying than Admiral ALRXEIEFF'S pro- clamation to the Manchurians? Manchuria is a Chinese province, China is neutral in the present war. Therefore proclaims the Admiral, all Chinese officials in Manchuria, "instead of hinder- ing, must assist the Russian Army." The people are to be held responsible for tile safety of the railway and the telegraph lines, and extermin- ated without mercy if they "give evi- dence of hatred" to the Russian troops. And this monstrous order to a population which is not legally subject to the CZAR is issued in the name of a Government, which just had the effrontery to denounce Japan for violat- ing the laws of civilised nations. Here is a Power, which calls itself civilised, threatening to repeat the massacre of Blagovestchenck, where 12,000 Chinese men,women and children were deliberately murdered by the savage Cossacks of the peaceful CZA.R. There does not seem much prospect that Admiral ALEXEIEFF will harm the Japanese,but he can always have the glory of butchering the Chinese peasantry who refuse to defend the Russian Rail- way. His proclamation is only one more symptom of the chaos that reigns in the governing class of Russia, for it is not likely that the St. Petersburg Foreign Office when it addressed to the Powers the protest against the action of Japan in Korea had the slightest idea that they would be made still more grotesque by the lawless frenzy OfALEXEIEFF in Manchuria. The world now sees Russia as she really is, and is not going to cherish any more illusions about her strength or her capacity for civilization.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield practically opened his elec- tion campaign on Thursday evening when he de- livered a thoughtful and statesmanlike speech to the members of the Liseral Club. From now henceforth the work of organising the forces of the party and consolidating the rank and file of the Liberal electoran will proceed without intermission. Next week Mr. Cros- field will entertain the workers for the great bazaar to be held in October next, while on the 9th of March he will be present at a Liberal demonstration in Parr Hall to be address- ed by Mr. Howes, of the National Reform Union, and probably Sir John Brunner. In the mean- time it is important and interesting to note that Mr. Alexander Wilkie has declined the overtures of the Labour Representation Committee and definitely decided not to contest the Borough in the Labour interest. Very unwisely, in our view, and with small prospect of success, the Committee has decided to again approach the headquarters of Labour in London with a view to securing a Labour candi- date for Warrington. It is only a month or two sines we dealt fully with the question of Labour representation and pointed out the folly of the Labour Representation Committee in forcing a three cornered contest in 'Warrington, by which they stood to gain nothing and lose practically all they had, for there is 110 question that, as we stated on December 12th, in Mr. Crosfield they will find a man who is prepared to go a very long way with them towards the goal they desire to reach." In the course of his speech on Thursday evening Mr. Crosfield made the gratifying announcement that he had conferred with some of the most responsible and able Trades Union leaders in the town, with the result that they saw their way to work shoulder to shoulder with him in defence of all the legitimate de- mands of Trades Unions. Mr.Crosfield also declared his intention of giving Mr. Shackleton's Bill his whole-hearted support, so that there is no reason why he should not be looked upon almost as much a Labour as a Liberal candidate. This new and definite declaration of policy on Mr. Crosfield's part will do much to strengthen his position and render the misguided efforts of the Labour Repre- sentation Committee still more unnecessary and futile.

Some of the great Tory jugglers were occupying the boards at Parr Hall on Thursday evening and displaying their little tricks to a more or less admiring multitude. They had prepared a special programme for workingmen, who were invited by poster and placard, if they wanted fall employ- ment and good wages, to visit Parr Hall and "learn how." Whether they were any wiser after the event is open to doubt, for, specious and plausible as the arguments of the forensic jug- glers were, there was nevertheless an undercurrent of feeling in the minds of the workers that the new panacea for all the industrial evils might not be so effective in practice as it was made to appear in theory. Distrust of the Government—of Joseph and his brethi en,—has permeated the ranks even of the most loyal "true blues." They do not like the signs of the times and there was con- sequently a lack of real enthusiasm during Thurs- day evening's performance. Even an imposing platform of Primrose Leaguegrande dames did not inspire the necessary confidence, and many a listener probably wondered how many of these good ladies were dependent upon vested interests in some form or other. As friends of the workingmen they did not somehow appear to the best advantage.

We are glad to note that Mr. R. Pierpoint has made his position in regard to the fiscal question quite clear by declaring himself a follower of Mr. Chamberlain, and a " whole. hogger.' By doing so he leaves himself no loophole for escape, and the probabilities are that when the general election comes round he will regret not having hedged a little, as so many Conservative candi- dates are doing. To be associated with an unpopular and discredited cause is not an en- viable position to be in but it is one we can see Mr. Pierpoint occupying in the near future. There is nothing in the local member's speech of Thurs- tiay evening to indicate that he is at all conversant with the merits or demeiits of Free Trade. The nature of his arguments shows rather that he is bitten with the grand imperial idea of " Free Trade within the Empire" and stern seclusion from the rest of the commercial world, for he talks in round resonant generalities of the " Unity of the Empire," and " Preference to the Colonies," at the same time assuring his hearers that Mr. Chamberlain's proposals would not "affect by one farthing for the worse the food bill of any man in this country." Are we to take Mr. Pierpoint's zpse diit on this point ? Emphatically No ! Why, Mr Cnamberlain himself admits that the cost of living under his scheme will be greater, but he takes care to add that work and wages will in- crease in proportion, which, however, at the same time he fails to prove. How is it that Mr. Pier- point goes so far as to deny the possibility of any enhanced price of food when Mr. Chamberlain admits the contrary? Apparently our member is another of Joseph's brethren "in a hole." If the workingmen of Warrington are prepared to swallow statements of this kind then they are more easily gulled than we could have imagined. The probabilities are otherwise. One constituency after another is seeing the hollowness of the Chamberlain phantasy and we do not believe that Warrington will go to the poll with its eyes closed when the times comes.

Sir Howard Vincent painted the picture of our c‘nnmercial position very black indeed—in fact his audience must have involuntarily congratulated themselves on being able to eke out an existence at all. He could speak on behalf of his own con- stituents, he said. "For many weeks past,upwards of three thousand penniless families in Shaffield had been dependent on public subscription and philanthropy for one meal a day." All due to the blighting effect of Free Trade, of course. Let us look backward a little, the process is instructive. Protectionists and "Fair Traders" want to impose a tax on the importation of wheat in the interests of agriculture. In 1815, the nation had a bitter experience of this kind of Protection, for in that year a Corn Law was enacted and remained in operation until 1846. During these thirty years the landlords thrived and the people starved. Sheffield had 20,000 and Leeds had 30,000 people dependent on the rates, while whole families were compelled to live on bran. How does the • gallant Colonel reconcile these facts with his present pro- posals? In 1904 under Free Trade, there is not a man, woman or child who is not better off than he or she would have been under the old system which was called Protection, and which we are advised to return to. With such facts as these before them it will b 3 a difficult matter for "whole- hoggers ".such as Colonel Sir Howard Vincent and Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., to induce people to vote for putting a duty on foreign wheat i.e., levying a bread tax, or to support any such reactionary proposals as those which have originated in the fertile brain of the ex-Colonial Secretary.

There is one thing we can honestly congratulate Sir Howard Vincent upon, and that is his courage in boldly supporting the Proposed introduction of Chinese Labour into South Africa. He tells us it is absolutely necessary that labour " at present unobtainable should be introduced into South Africa for the development of her natural resources." The italics are ours. ; We could laugh, were the issues not so grave and full of peril and shame for this coßntry. Sir Howard complains bitterly of the aliens—the scum of the earth—invading thi country and driving English workmen out of ern ' ployrnent.With the same breath he approves of the introduction of yellow labour, under cunditions tantamount to slavery itself, into a British colony after we have spent 250 millions of pounds and sacrificed thousands of lives with the ostensible aim of relieving the oppressed Outlander. What a ghastly farce it all is ! Tommy Atkins will turn in his grave when he hears the soft tread of the heathen Chinee on the land he gave life itself to make free. This crowning infamy has sure y sounded the death knell of an iniquitous govern ment which has all along kow-towed to the " pe3 iferous cosmopolitans" of the Rand.

Speaking of Conservative demonstrations, we Tecollect a time—not very far back—when the •Junior Consarvatives of Warrington were glorying in the prospect of a visit from Mr. Winston Churchill, M.P. We are still anxiously awaiting the appearance of this militant young politician •on a Parr Hall platform. At the present juncture his views on the fiscal question would be of 'particular interest to a Warrington audience, and the Junior Conservatives would be doing a useful service in making immediate arrangements for the fulfilment of the engagement. Perhaps Lord Hugh Cecil could be induced to accompany Mr. Churchill. Whether their ideas on bad trade and its remedy would accord with those so confi- dently put forward by Sir Howard Vincent and Mr. Robert Piernoint,is, of course,another matter. 'Sir Howard Vincent may, however, be more effectively answered in his own constituency, if Mr. Churchill contests the Central division of Sheffield.

Four candidates have entered the lists for the appointment of two elective auditors. Politics in this election are eschewed, but for many years the auditors elected were connected with the two political parties and the honours were equally divided. This arrangement was upset a year ago on the resignation of Mr. John Harrison who has been so long identified with the Liberal party. He Was succeeded: by Mr. Laurence Bolton, who had the support of the Non-Trading Association, hnd the Liberals were left without a representative auditor. This year there is another innovation. The Labor party have brought forward a candi- date in Mr. James Ball, who goes to the poll in company with three professional accountants. There appears to be an impression that an unpro- f.essionalman like Mr.Ball,who by the way is a clerk in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee, Would prove a useful change from the approved type. It is a somewhat singular fact that the revelations which have been made by elective auditors at Manchester, Liverpool, and Stockport, have all been made by non-professional gentlemen.

LOCAL NEWS.

Personal. Mr. Charles Elijah Place, son of Mr. Elijah Place, Markets -inspector and inspector of weights and measures, has successfully passed the examination recently held by the Board of Trade in accordance With section 11, of the Weights and Measures Act, 1889, and in due course will receive the certificate of qualification. Mr. C. E. Place has been an assistant markets' inspector and assistant inspector of weights and medsures at Warrington, since October, 1901. FIRE GRATES of every description.—J. S. GAR- LAND, Furnishing . Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, 'Bridge-street, Warrmgion.—Advt. Madame Clara Butt's Visit. Madame Clara Butt, Mr. Kennerley Rumford, and a party of distinguished artistes will give a concert in the Parr Hall on Thursday, March 10th.

St. Ann's Vicarage: On Saturday afternoon next, th 3 Bishop of Liver- pool will unveil the Queen Victoria memorial tablet in the new St. Ann's Vicarage, Orford Avenue. For full particulars see Advt.

The Bewsey Ward Vacancy. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee, on Monday afternoon, the Town Clerk reported that he had received a copy of the c)nviction of Mr. Samuel Gough at Liverpool Assizes on a charge of bribery and thatMr.Gough had ceased to be a member of the Council. There was no discussion. We under 'stand that any two burgesses in the ward can ask for the vacancy to be filled up. Address to Men. At the Parish Church la3t evening, the Ven- Archdeacon Madden delivered an address to men on ' The marks of the Jesus," pointing out that although Christians were not now called upon to suffer perse- cution for religion, they could bear the marks of Christ, such as purity and temperance, in their charac- ters—Next Friday evening the Rev. John Wakeford will speak on "Man in his completeness."

Contract for a Local Tobacconist. Messrs. T. Sudlow and Company, wholesale and retail cigar merchants, Winwick-street, Warrington, have succeeded in obtaining the contract for tobacco, cigars and cigarettes required at Orford Barracks; the heddquarters of the 8-40th regimental district. Fall From a Window. An accident, which might have resulted seriously, befel Mrs. Julia Huxley, a widow, residing at 142, Knutsford-road, at one o'clock on Wednesday after- noon. She was cleaning a bedroom window at the back of the house when she became faint, and fell a distance of 12 feet into the yard. Dr. Smith was called in and found her to be suffering from a fractured Wrist and shock. She is progressing satisfactorily. BacE BOOTS FOR ALL.—UNDER THE CLOCK.—Advt Local Labour Matters.
