ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS VIDIERAL WATERS.—AdVt. Fire Brigade's Trying Experience. Fighting the flames is at all times an arduous task, 13nt, the Warrington Fire Brigade had a parti- cularly trying experience during the fierce gale which raged on Sunday night. At 10.35 a telephonic message was received that a fire had broken out on the premises of Mr. James Hepherd, Boundary Farm, Woolston. The brigade turned out in charge of Deputy-superintendent F. H. Turner and pro- ceeded with all haste to the scene of the fire, which is three! miles from Warrington. A thirty-ton stack of hay was well alight, and it was feared that the sparks would ignite an adjoining sack. /A good supply of water Nva obtained from a brook and the firemen got to work with two power- fill jets and the steamer" Major." Fortunately they succeeded in cmfining the fire to the one stack. After about two hours' work, the pumps of the engine be- Caine choked and this necessitated a stoppage or some considerable time. The wind was %plowing a hurricane, and to add to the r9,,'scomfort of the firemen, rain and sleet fell heavily. -uhe stack was completely destroyed, and the damage, ,v,bich is covered by insurance, is estimated at• MO. brigade returned to Warrington at ten o'clock on alcllday morning, aud the deputy-superintendent told our representative that during the whole of his twenty Years experience he had never before been called to 11 fire on such a night.

Death of Mr. Richard Toft. We regret to record the death of Mr. Richard Toft, tobacconist, Horsemarket-street and Sankey-street, who passed away on Saturday morning at the age of 74 years. The deceased gentleman had been suffer- ing from bronchitis for a considerable time, but the end came very suddenly. He had carried on the business of a tobacconist for about forty years, and was esteemed and respected by a very large circle of friends. He was a worshipper at St. Paul's Church, and the Vicar (Rev. W. Bracecamp) made a fitting allusion to his death, at Sunday evening's service. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon, the Rev. W. Bracecamp officiating. A large gathering of relatives and friends witnessed the last rites including Mr. Harold Toft (grandson). Mr. E. Aukland, Mr. E. Aukland, jun., Mr. F. Aukland, Alderman Smethurst, Mr. A. Jolley, and Mr. Denham. A large number of floral tributes were placed over the • grave.

FOR CLASS AND CHrNA. of every description try J. S. GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. The Late Mr. Thomas Tunstall.

At a meeting of the managers of the Warrington Savings Bank, held on Saturday, reference was made to the painfully sudden death of Air. Thomas Tunstall, head of the firm of Messrs. T. and J. L. Tunstall and Company, chartered accountants, and actuary at the bank. It was decided to forward a letter of condol- ence with Mrs. Tunstall and family in their bereave- ment.—The Rev. W. Bracecamp made a fitting allusion to the late Mr. Tunstall in his ser- mon at St. Paul's Church on Sunday evening. They had, he said, lost a man that Warrington could' ill afford to lose. Men of Mr. Tunstall's stamp and character were, he was sorry to say, rare. He was an exemplary business man, and, those who worked with him in that capacity would miss his advice and wisdom. He was a gool Churchman, and his attend- ance and ' usefulness at St. Paul's would be long remembered.

Memorial Service at the Spiritualist Hall. At the Spiritualists' Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday evening a memorial service to the late Mr. Pemberton who for a number of years had been a prominent member and worker in connection with the society was held. There was a crowded attendance. The service was taken by Mr. J. B. Tetlow, of Manchester, a late fellow worker of Mr. Pemberton's. The Presi, dent of the society, Mr. W. E. Stout, paid a personal tribute to their departed friend. Mr. Tetlow also referred to his long acquaintance with Mr. Isemberton stating that he was present 25 years ago, at the open- ing of the first Spiritualist Lyceum at Blackburn, of which Mr. Pemberton was founder. He spoke of Mr. Pemberton's mediumship, and stated that during the many years he had used it, it had proved to be a benefit in many cases physically, and in all cases mentally. and spiritually. On the motion of the President, seconded and supported respectively by Mr. Nightingale and Mr. J. Foster, sea., the audience rose in silence and passed a vote of sympathy witll the bereaved family. GARLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, 51, 53, and 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Presentation to a Workman. There was an interesting little ceremony at the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., Bank Quay, at noon on Saturday, when Mr. Robert Fitzgerald, an employe who is afflicted with paralysis, was presented with a self-propelling chair, subscribed for by his fellow-workmen.—Captain G. R. Crosfield made the presentation, and expressed sympathy with Mr. Fitzgerald in his misfortune. It would be a source of very great happiness to him to know that his comrades all through the works had contributed towards the chair, with the greatest heartiness and goodwill.—Mr. T. Rigby returned thanks on behalf of Mr. Fitzgerald, and pointed out that the presentation was first suggested at the annual meeting of the sick club. Thirty men were appointed to solicit subscrip- tions from the employes, and the result was that £25 7s. 2d. was• contributed. The cost of the chair was 19 guineas and it was intended to erect a shed in which it could be placed, and to provide other little requisites.—On the proposition of Mr. Kendrick, seconded by Mr. Randles, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Captain Crosfield.—ln responding,Captain Crosfield said that so lona as comrades would come forward and help one who was in misfortune there was not much wrong with the English workman.

H. M. ASHTON'S- LIE% ToisTro is the KING or TONICS.-63. SA.NKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. The Farmer's Claim for Compensation. A meeting in connection with the Cheshire Milk Producers Association was held at the Warrington Corn Exehange, on Wednesday, Mr. Thomas Baxter, of Dutton Hall, presiding over a good attendance of farmers. Mr. James Sadler, secretary of the associa- tion, delivered an address on "The compulsory slaughter of tuberculous cattle and the farmer's claim for compensation." He alluded to the bill which the Manchester Corporation were trying to get through Parliament,which would empower them to slaughter cows affected with tuberculous udder, without compen- sating the farmers. A. deputation from that Associa- tion had waited upon the Corporation and requested them to insert a clause in the Bill that farmers should be compensated for the slaughter of cattle. He con- tended that no authority should have the power to demand the slaughter of a beast in the public interest unless provision was made for the payment of com- pensation. The adulteration of milk in Cheshire had got to such a low point, that itWas now almost extinct. This he attributed to the fact that the Cheshire farmers sent out genuine milk. Mr. Sadler advocated the claims of the Association; and appealed to non- members to join it.

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. Memorial to Fallen Soldiers. Warrington is to have a fitting memorial to the men of the South Lancashire Regiment (Line, Militia, and Volunteers) who fell in the South African campaign. The first list of subscriptions to this object, which must appeal forcibly to the townspeople, appears in another column. In a circular issued by the Mayor, on behalf of the Executive Committee, it is stated: "The form of the proposed memoridl has not been definitely settled, for much will depend won the amount collected; but it has been suggested that the memorial might take the form of a portrait statue of the late Lieutenant-Colonel MacCarthy O'Leary, who lost his life while gallantly leading his men in the final charge on Pieter's Hill, and, which charge i cleared the way for the relief of Ladysmith. It s proposed to place tablets on the base of the memorial recording by battalions the names of those who died on service during the War. The Memorial will be erected in a public place in War- rington, the County home of the Regiment. Towards the sum of £1,500 which it is estimated will be required to carry out the wishes of the Committee, the officers and men of the South Lancashire Regi- ment have subscribed over MK Subscriptions may be paid into any of the Banks, to the Hon. Treasurer, Mr. John Fairhurst, Municipal Offices, Sankey-street; or to any member of the Committee." A print of the picture by W. B. Wollen, R. 1., of "The South Lanca- shires Storming the Boer Trenches at Pieter's Hill," printed on good paper, will be presented to all sub- scribers of 2s. 6d. and upwards. A print of the picture and a copy of a booklet entitled The South Lancashire Regiment in the South African War," containing a portrait of the late Lieut.-Col. W. MacCarthy O'Leary, and several other illustrations, will be presented to subscribers of ss. and upwards. A print of the picture, with wide margin suitable for framing, and a copy of the booklet will be presented to all subscribers of V. is. and upwards.—A public meeting will be held at the Town Hall on Thursday evening for the purpose of appointing an auxiliary or workingmen's committee to raise funds. -

The Examiner, Saturday, February 27, 1904 MR.CROSFIELD SPEAKS OUT.

!County Council Elections. • There will be no contests for County Council seats in the Warrington district. Yesterday was the last day for receiving nominations, and Mr. Roger C. Parr was returned unopposed for the Appleton Division, and Mr. J. W. Knowles for Winwick. Socialists, and the Fiscal Question. Mr. Fred Bramley, of Bradford, delivered a lecture at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, last evening, under the auspices of the 1.L.P., on "Socialism and the Fiscal Question." He weighed the arguments of Mr. Chamberlain and the Free Traders and said that Socialists were neither Protectionists nor Free Traders in the orthodox sense. Their contention was that an attempt should be made to break down internal monopolies and the restriction of industrial progress. —To-morrow (Sunday) evening Mr. Bramley will lecture on "The Ethics of Socialism," and the Rev. H. Dunnico will preside. Presentations to Conservatives. . • Last evening a smoking concert was held at the Junior Conservative Club, Mr.. E. Draper presiding over a large gathering. The principal event was the presentation of a silver tea and coffee service, eight day clock, smoker's companion, and an illuminated address, to Mr. W. L. Williams (chairman of the club), on the occasion of his marriage. ,Mrs. Pierpoint made the presentation on behalf of the committee and mem- bers. The prizes gained in the recent billiard handi- cap were presented by Lady Greenall, Mr. Stangl being the winner of the Parr Challenge Cup. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., and other prominent Conservatives were present. Lecture on "Electricity." At the Old Academy last evening a very interesting lecture on " Electricity " was delivered by Mr, W. H. Grimsdale, who made special reference to the development of electricity in Warrington during the past three years. He described the methods employed in the production of electricity, and gave a number of experiments. Mr. F. W. Monks presided, and pointed out that at present keen competition was going on between, electricity, the motor steam engine (including the steam turbine) and the gas engine. It was difficult at the moment to say which was likely to win but personally he thought the gas engine might be looked upon as likely to come out on the top and that with a properly arranged and constructed gas plant, driving suitable engines, electricity could be produced at a lower cost per unit than they were able to produce it at Niagara with water turbines. Distribution of Billiard Prizes. At the Conservative Club on Wednesday evening the last round in the billiard handicap was played,Mr. W. Davies defeating Alderman Francornb. Afterwards Mr. E. Draper distributed the prizes as follows W. Davies marble clock; 2, Alderman Francomb, fruit stand; 3, C. Oldfield, fish carvers ; 4, R. Tickle, cigarette case ; T. Lsngshaw, case of carvers; Thomas Horrobin, jun., jam dish; W. E. Baylin, match box; and F. Ward, butter cooler, Consolation prizes :—l, J. Thompson, barometer; 2, George Smith, teapot; 3, H. Hughes, cue and case; 4, Sergeant Major Rawlinsoir, pipe.; H. Watkins, spirit flask ; J. Hesketh, pipe; and E. Fowler, box of cigars. The Work of Warrington Artists. Two further additions will shortly be made to the collection of pictures by Warrington artists in the local art gallery. At a meeting of the General Pur- poses Committee held on Monday, it was decided to purchase two landscapes by Mr. James Charles, the son of a Warrington tradesman. Mr. Charles is an Associate of the French Academy and has gained a high reputation as an artist. The pictures which have just been purchased are two of the finest examples of his work in landscape painting. One is entitled "The Picnic," in which the members of his family are seen disport- ing themselves in an orchard. The other picture gives an excellent cattle scene, with a fine sky over- head as a distinguishing feature.—lt is intended to hold an exhibition of Hr. Charles's work in the art gallery at an early date. Warrington Choirs at Widnes. NATIONAL SERIVCE LEAGUE. This project raises many issues which we have not time to discuss to-night,but there can be no doubt that a hundred days military training for a man, besides qualifying him for the defence of his country and his home in time of need, would be of physical benefit to him. (Cheers.) ' Turning for a moment to another aspect of the ques- tion it is equally clear that a powerful well equipped auxiliary force is the most economical way of defend- ing these shores against the possibility of invasion. (Hear hear.) I have had many talks with my brother here on the matter and he knows how I have clung and for the matter of that, still cling to a solution on voluntary lines. But I must frankly confess that the difficulties in the way of it are very great indeed. Only to men- tion one ef them, it is unfair to handicap a just and patriotic employer of labour, who will allow his managers, clerks, foremen and artisans—a fair propor- tion of them—to attend camp for the stipulated time where he competes with his less public spirited neighbour who will have nothing to do with it. What is a still more serious difficulty, is the danger of employ& who wish to serve their country being cold shouldered Or penalised, on one pretext or another " sacked " by employers who resent their absence while on military drill. If you will look into this very urgent problem you will find the difficulties of a solution on voluntary Hines are very serious. For my part I still keep an open mind, though I am taking steps to get the best opinions one can in various quarters. (Hear hear). MIS POSITION WITH REGARD TO LABOUR.

Meeting at the Liberal Club.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield attended a meeting at the Liberal Club on Thursday evening for the purpose of pre- senting the prizes to the successful competitors in the recent billiard handicap. Before doing so, Mr. Crosfield delivered a vigorous address on the political topics of the hour. Rarely has he been found in better form and his direct pronouncement on the labour movement was greatly appreciated. Mr. Councillor Tinnion presided and he was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Alderman Pierpoint, Captain Cros- field, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes, J. Harrison, J. Worrall, J. T. Locker W. Furness, T. Pemberton, and others. In the course of a few introductory remarks, the Chairman observed that the: present time was one in which Liberals and Progressives should meet together and kill,as far as they could,the reactionary measures of the present Government. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19040227_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEECH BY MR. A. H. CROSFIELD.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who bad a hearty reception, said: "I have to thank you in the first place for ask- ing me to perform this pleasant little duty—the dis- tribution of prizes. It is a duty I have had the plea- sure of taking part in many a time—(hea,r, hear)—and on these occasions we have generally had a little talk about billiards and also about bowls. You know that I handle a cue myself sometimes—(hear, hear)—in fact a good many of our family have rather a bent that way. (Hear, hear.) There is an amusing little anecdote in connection with billiards which I may have mentioned to you before, which occurred before my time. It takes us back to the great days of reform \iwhen Cobden and Bright were fighting shoulder to shoulder. My Uncle George was a keen politician and a good deal in touch with them both and they used to come sometimes to Walton Lea. One evening after they had finished discussing politics Richard Cobden and my unclehad a set to at billiards and my Uncle gave him a good hammering. But Cobden turned the tables on him all the same by quietly remarking " Ah, Crosfield, what a deal of time you must have wasted on this game ! " (Laughter.) Well lam very glad indeed to hear from my friend Mr. Pember- ton that the billiard handicap has been such a thorough success, and I am sure every one in this room knows how much the great success of this club in recent years is due to his constant energy and activity. (Cheers).

===== 0002601_19040227_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ADVANTAGES OF RECREATION.

I have sometimes given you a disquisition on bowls, at which I'm a duffer, but in which I take a great interest. I shall not devote much time to sports now, for reasons I shall give you in a moMent, but I may just say what I think I have said before in this club that I have never advocated the healthy recreation that you get in your outdoor game of bowls in the summer, and billiards in the winter, nor any other kind of sport, merely as an end in itself. (Hear, hear.) I do advocate these things, though for the sake of the health, the physique, and the enjoyment of thousands of workingmen in the town: (Cheers) and because I think English games have had not a little to do with the formation of English charac- ter. The friendly contests in our playgrounds equip us for the sterner battle of life; teach us to hit out straight from the shoulder if need be, but never below the belt. (Hear, hear.) I have in my pocket now a letter from apolitical opponent, but a very good friend of mine—(hear, hear,) Mr, Edward Taylor, who tells me that the bowling league of Warrington is to be revived and recognised. It includes every important bowling cliff) in the town, and I am glad to know it includes this club too Mr. Chairman. And so what Mr. Taylor calls this fine healthy sport is to be further en- couraged by the revival of this league and lam very, glad of it. (Cheers.).Of course we all know there is a happy!mean in everything and games may be overdone, but I am thankful to know that so many of our fellow- townsmen get their recreations in the summer in this way, and I think they are a good deal better off playing a game like bowls or billiards than wasting time,—if I may say so—in reading the pink halfpenny paper and "spotting "—I believe they call it spottinz (laughter) the winners, and I am afraid occasionally backing losers. (Renewed laughter and cheers.)

===== 0002601_19040227_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POLITICAL SITUATION REVIEWED.

Now at these friendly gatherings it has been custom- ary to take stock of the political situation, and the Chairman has invited me to do so, but that is not what I propose to do to-night. I rather wish we had a tele- phone to another hall in the town, or rather a mega- phone. It would be interesting to hear what is being said there because a little daylight might be thrown on the difficulties of this great controversy, and though I rather fear it will scarcely be illuminated or elucidated by what we shall hear from that distinguished speaker Sir 'Howard Vincent. Now as I say, lam not going to speak at any length on Free Trade. We have had some eloquentand powerful speeches in Warrington by Mr. Howes—(hear, hear)—who very truly said the next election was net to be fought on Free Trade alone. If it had been fought on Free Trade and nothing else, that in itself is sufficient to rally every thinking working- man who feels a proper sense of responsibility and duty to the progressive cause, and to induce every man to take an active share in the work. (Hear, hear.) I advise you to read the speeches made to-night. in Parr Hall and just see if you!can get an answer to the questions that we have put time after time—questions which have never been answered either by any of the Conservative or so called Unionist leaders, or any of the minor luminaries of our opponents who are spread- ing their protectionist propaganda. They are putting out, bills promising more employment. (Laughter.) That is one of the very points on which we want enlightenment. It is admitted that if you place a pro- tectionist duty on any article, the duty is almost entirely paid by the consumer. (Hear, hear.) It has been shown perfectly plainly that there will be an increased cost in not only food but all essential articles, as well as in little luxuries consumed by the people, and we know that the home market is some seven or eight times greater in bulk of the export tracle.Therefore if by inereasrng the cost of articles you

REIRICE THE PURCHASING POWER- OF A SOVEREIGN to say seventeen shillings amongst the 42 million in- habitants of this county you get a reduced consumption and if you get a reduzed connumption, there is less manufactured and less demand for labour. (Hear, hear.) What we ask is are employers going to be able to increase the wages of their employes when the de- mand for labour is reduced? That is an all important point. It has not been answored because it cannot be answered. (Hear, hear.) Another question on which i great stress will be laid s

EDUC AT (ON.

I was talking the other day to Praessor Lodge, whose 'departure from the North of England to the Midlands we all regret. You know he is an eminent man of science, but naturally and properly impartial in 'politics. I had been condemning the Government's education policy. "Well" he replied, "have you got your own scheme cut and dried as an alternative?'' No gentlemen, but thereare one or two points on which we Liberals have made up our minds and on which we shall insist when we come to revise this bungle and mess which the Government have made of the matter. (Hear, hear.) We shall also sweep away the scandalous anachronism of tests. (Cheers). They are entirely out of date and it really is monstrous that more than half the population of the country should be debarred from the honorable profession of teachers in schools through narrow sectarian tests. It is of course equally unfair that the honourable and coveted position ofhea,d- master in a school should be closed to a large number of people for the same reason. (Hear, hear.) All this is not only injustice but dead loss to the whole nation because people who have the strength of character and principle to resist sectarian tests are often the very people who would be most efficient and valuable as teachers. There is a third matter which I have been glad to see brought forward of late, and that is the

===== 0002601_19040227_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
TAXATION OF LAND VALUES.

(Hear, hear.) Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman has recently urged this very forcibly, so has Mr. Asquith and Sir Edward Grey, Mr. Charles Trevelyan and a good many others. Well we have talked about this taxation of land values and ground rents for a good many years, and we shall now have to tackle it in real earnest. (Cheers). We are not advocates. no fair minded Englishman can be an advocate for spoliation. What we want is not spoliation but justice—(hear, hear)— and in the name of justice I submit that a revision of our system of land taxation which has been pressing for many years, is now more pressing and urgent than ever because of the great, the enormous increase of our national expenditure. When the Liberals get into power a few months or it may be a few weeks hence

ECONOMY

will have to be, on of the first questions taken in hand. There are many other questions we know of pressinr, importance which ought to be dealt with. One of the very first is Old Age Pensions, which Mr. Chamberlain has dangled so long before the electorate. But we have got to find the money for it, and economy alone will not do that. No, we shall have to tap fresh sources of revenue, and the most important of these is the tax- ationof land values. (Cheers.) This is essential for purposes of revenue, but it is still more important from the point of view of the physique of the nation, because until this reform is passed it will be practically impossible for us to in out the terribly overcrowded centres of population in our great cities. (Hear, hear.) I have read something on this question of the housing of the poor, and it seems to me that until we have dealt with this, reform by the taxation of land values all attempts to solve the housing problem will be so much tinkering. We do not want to banish workmen into lonely country, but we do want to get them away from their work of an evening into suburban surroundings, where life will be healthier and pleasanter and better for all concerned. (Hear, hear.) I am glad that you recognise the importance of that from the point of view of the physique of the nation. MR. SHACKLETON'S DILL.

and I am very anxious to take this opportunity of cor- recting once and for all any misapprehension on that point. lam thoroughly in sympathy with Mr. Shackl- eton's Bill. (Hear, hear.) During the last fortnight I have had the very great privilege and advantage of meeting some of the most responsible and able leaders of Trades Unionism in Warrington. and f told these gentlemen what I tell you know, that I recognise as fully as any man here the necessity for proper organi- sation of labour—(hear, hear)—directed to maintain a proper standard of life and to secure a fair share of the result of industry and commerce—(hear, hear)—and I will venture to say further that I hope and believe that if the leaders of these important societies, with some of whom I have spent so many hours lately, were with us to-night they would tell you that they and I very well see our way to working shoulder to shoulder in the defence of all the legitimate objects of Trade Unonism. (Loud cheers.) The revision of the law as it stands now is a matter that touches the interests of the workingman very closely indeed • and there is another matter to which Jam going to allude which I also feel pretty certain will touch them to the quick. It is per- haps the most re-actionary proceeding of a Government, which I notice in this morning's paper Dr. Guiness Rogers describes as the most re-actionary he has ever known in all his long life. (Hear, hear.) By the way gentlemen, in passing, the "Westminster Gazette," or rather Mr. F. C. 4. hits off the position very happily in yesterday's paper. Some of you may have seen his most amusing picture. (Hear, hear.) The sphinx in Egypt is looking at Joseph who has left Westminster to take some well earned recreation, and the sphinx says, "Can you tell me the difference between yourself and the Joseph who ,came here some thousands of years ago ?" Joe looks puzzled for a bit, and then says "No, I'll give it up. "Well," says the sphinx, "That Joseph's brethren put him into a hole; you've put your brethren into one." (Laughter.)

CHINESE LABOUR. Gentlemen, the matter to which I was going to allude is one which I think will prove the proverbial last straw that will break the elentorate's back. You will remember a very few years since the sacrifices you were asked to make on behalf of the oppressed Outlander. Oppressed was the wrong word: There were serious grievances but on the whole he had not such bad time it, and while earning a pound a day in the mines was on the whole perhaps better off than any other miner in the world. Gentlemen, I am not going to revive old differences about the war. We may" bury that hatchet "now, though it is impossible to ignore its ruinous consequences. But there is one question that everyone without distinction is now likely to ask and that is whether we spent two hundred, and fifty millions of money, sacrificed the lives of twenty thousand British soldiers, yes and the lives of twenty thousand women and children who suffered agony and miserable deaths in the Transvaal in order that after all Chinese labour is to be imported to do the work that ought to be done by British subjects. (Cheers). After qnoting extracts from the speeches of Sir Henry Fowler and Mr. John Barns, Mr. Crosfield added: All I can say is that after the lies that have been concocted during the last ten years by these magnates on the Rand, and their truly unscrupulous deception of the British public, I for one shall take with a mighty big grain of salt any tales they send to England for home consumption, or as likely as not manufacture them on the spot here. (Hear, hear.) [have mentioned some of the chief objects we shall have to fight for when the time comes, and it may come very soon indeed. Whather it be in a few weeks or a few months it is not possible that a Government so utterly dis- credited canhold together much longer. The debate both on the Chinese labour ftnestion and on Free Trade has damaged them fatally. (Elear, hear.) It is all very well for members of Parliament to come down to War- rington to expound Protectionist doctrines, whieh so far as this country is concerned were exploded sixty years ago. They may stump on the platform when they have it to themselves, but what sort of figure did they cut when Free Traders and Protectionists met each other face to face in the HOLM of Commons the other day in the most brilliant debate there has been for years. (Hear, hear.) Gentlemen they cut no figure at all

• THEY SIMPLY SCUTTLED AWAY from the Protectionist position and sought shelter under a meaningless formula. Well recognising how politic- ally demoralised our opponants are, workingmen friends of mine—and I am proud to say that many of the best' friends I have in Warrington are workingmen—(cheers) they corhe to me from time to time complaining that our party is handicapped because our organiza- tion is inferior to the organization of the Conservative party. V Gentlemen in reply to that I would first respsetf ally observe that personally I never accepted responsibility for the working of the Party machine because I already have my hands very I full without that. Secondly if there is any ground for these complaints, the shortcomings of our organisation are to some extent and partially dim to the fact that the money bags are most of them on the other side. (Laughter.) In the third place even if there are any little defects in

OUR ORGANISATION to be repaired, does not the remedy after all lie chiefly in your own hands. (Hear, hear.) Is not this a time when every Captain and every Secretary in the wards should feel the weight of responsibility upon him rest- ing on ,his !individual efforts between now and the election. (Hear, hear.) We were talking just now of auxiliary forces; why not auxiliary political forces. (Hear, hear.) We want a little more public spirited work and if we get this backing our victory *ill be an absolute certainty (A voice : 1830). We have a still greater cause VO fight for than in the year 1880 my friend speaks of—a cause thlt may well draw to the Progressive Party every man vriL. a shred of Liberalism and love of progress in him welding us all together in one solid phalanx, from th'e Liberal Unionist at one end to the extreme Radical at the other end of the party. (Cheers). The justice of our °lase is overwhelming, and that is why I think at this juncture we may fairly and respect- fully put it t 9 the intelligent artisans of our town, who have at heart glod of the country, the maintenanee of their standard of life, the welfare of their families, the education of their children. I thirk we may fairly ask them to give us a hearty.vigorous British support that we may win for our noble cause a great and lasting victory. (Loud cheers.)

Cruet, J. Halliwell. 6. Wedgwood biscuit jar, H. Heyes. 7. Fruit dish, J. Groome. 8. Case of Carvers, S. J. Beaman. 9. Tea-pot, W. Whittaker. 10. Breakfast cruet, J. Rudd. 11. Pickle jar, J. Shaw. 12. Oik biscuit barrel, Sergeant F'. Sweeney. 13. Case of carvers, W. Ford. 14. Butter dish, W. Birch. 15. Jam dish, E. Somerville. 16. Match box, J. Toole.

During the evening, a short musical programme was submitted. Mr. J. Rabbitt's fine tenor voice was heard to advantage in several songs and he was loudly encored Mr. F. Fieldhouse was, also in good voice; and in re- sponding to a recall for his vigorous rendering of" The Soldier's Song," he gave "Reign Edward Reign." The instrumental portion of the programme was well sus- tained by Messrs. Ray, Balmford and Kendrick, who contributed two instrumental trios. Mr. Balmford also played with taste and expression a solo on the cello. The accompaniments were played by Mr. Kendrick. The humour of the evening was supplied by Mr. W. Dowling whose humorous songs were received with favour.

ALDERMAN PIERPOINT ASKS FOR A LIBERAL RALLY.

Alderman PrEuPortirr, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. A. H. Crostleld and the artistes who had assisted said it was a matter of very great pleasure to him to find himself in the old Liberal Club of Warrington. (Hear, hear.) Still it was a matter of regret to him that he missed so many of those old Liberal friends of his that he was in the habit of meeting there, years ago. That was one of the penalties of growing old. There were still some of those friends left who ought to have been there, but who were not, and he was living in hopes that before the next general election they would have the pleasure of welcoming them again, and have their hearty co-operation in the great work that was before them. (Hear, hear.) He

had listened with great pleasure to the remarks of Mr. Arthur Crosfield—a speech which in his (Alderman Pierpoint's) opinion marked a great epoch in his charac- ter. So far as he was able to judge it was one of the best and most statesmanlike speeches that Mr. Crosfield had ever made—(hear, hear)—and they were all able to congratulate him upon the progress that he was evidently making in addressing large public audiences. If they were fortunate enough—as he hoped they would be—to vote for Mr. Crosfield as their candidate at the next election, Mr. Crosfield would be able to reproduce to a larger Liberal audience some of the salient points of his address that night. (Hear, hear.) He (Alderman Pier- point) had known all the Crosflelds for a great number of years. He had not the pleasure of knowing Mr. Arthur Crosfield's grandfather but he had the privilege of remembering the great reputation he had as an earnest worker for Liberalism of the old days. They all knew the good work which Mr. Crosfield's father and his uncle had done for Liberalism in Warrington. The great strength and backbone of the Liberal party lay in the members of the Crosfield family and it was a matter of congratulation to him to find that they had still representatives of the Crosfield family who were pre- pared to do what they could to further the principles of Liberalism. (Applause.) The meeting which was being held that night in Parr Hall was one which he ventured to say was held on behalf of vested interests—(hear, hear)—vested interests existing at the present time or vested interests which they wished to promulgate. He only wished that the megaphone could have transmitted the speech of Mr. Crosfield to Parr Hall. (Hear, hear). He thought it would have controverted everything that the Tory party was saying. Vested interests were all very well but they would find, as a rule, that they militated against the great mass of the people of this country. tHear, hear). Wherever they heard of a vested interest they should look upon it with eyes of suspicion. He would ask them to form their own opinion and he believed they would come to a different conclusion from that which the Tory party wished them to arrive at. Referring to the prospects of the Liberal party, Alderman Pierpoint said it was io use having a few figureheads doing what they could. It was like beating the air unless the leaders were backed up by a large and enthusiastic body of workers on behalf of the cause for which they were fighting. The Liberal party in Warrington appeared to him to have gone to pieces and it would take a great effort on the part of each member of the Liberal party to bring about that cohesion and strength which was so necessary for suc- cess. If there were some who . could not contribute money they could at any rate work and use their influ- ence. He had always held that Warrington could be, won—and ought to be won—for Liberalism. (Applause.) The Liberal party was fighting for the greater good of the greater number, and if they remembered that it was part of their duty to take part in the struggle which that involved. At the last election the results were lament- able. They were very much what he anticipated The forces on that occasion were all against them, but at the next election the forces would be operating in their favour. It only required them to take occasion by the hand, to put their shoulder to the wheel, and to be true to their own interests, and they would have no difficulty in bringing about that condition of things politically in Warrington, that he had hoped for and worked for during many years. (Applause.) He would do whatever he would on behalf of the Liberal party as willingly as he did when he was younger. (Hear, hear and applause.) If his words could have any effect upon the younger members of the party he would be glad. He hoped they would, by their assistance do the best they could to help in the furtherance of Liberalism. He need not say anything to that meeting in favour of Mr. Arthur Crosfield. He was the right man in the right place. (Hear, hear.) As Mr. Crosfield's father was the backbone of the Liberal party in his (Alderman Pier- point's) younger days so he looked upon Mr. Arthur Crosfield as the backbone of the Liberal party to-day. If Mr. Crosfield was willing to take off his coat in this political battle, the least they could do was to take off their coats also. (Hear, hear.) The Liberal party he was assured wouldhave a majority at the next election. Many of the old friends of the Conservative party were against their own party. He did not look upon the last majority in Warrington as a true index of the relative strength of the two parties. The normal majority in his opinion, was 600, and all they had to do was to convert 400 waverers to the Liberal party in order to be victors on the next occasion. The declaration Mr. Crosfield had made with reference to the labour party had given him great pleasure and satisfaction. He hoped those remarks would be read in the newspapers for he felt they would have a great moral effect upon the political atmosphere of Warrington. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN, in seconding the vote of thanks said there were some members of the Liberal party who thought the force of Liberalism in Warrington was somewhat spent. He did not agree with that. They were not working ostentatiously but he c auld assure them they were working quietly and effectively. (Hear hear.) Mr. Crosfield had been doing a great deal of good work for Liberalism during the last few months, and had met the leaders of the various sections of the various labour organisations in Warrington. They found themselves in line on practically all points affecting labour. They had, perhaps, not done as much public work as they would like, but as Mr. Crosfield said, the money-bags were on the other side. It was proposed to hold a meeting on the 9th of March, at which addresses would be delivered by Sir John Brunner and Mr. A. Howes, whose recent addresses on the fiscal question were so greatly appreciated. The Chairman proceeded to refer to the healthy condition of the Liberal Club and contended that it ought to be a very strong force on behalf of Liberalism. He did not know of a political club that would compare more favourably with it and he was of opinion that if the members put their shoulders to the political wheel in the ensuing election they would win'a great grand triumph as they did in 1880. (Applause.) A vote was carried with acclamation.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD briefly acknowledged the com- pliment. He congratulated Mr. Pemberton on having got together such a galaxy of musical talent.Theadmir- able music had been a pleasure to listen to. He thought Warrington was by no means behind hand in musical talent—(hear, hear)—and he most heartily endorsed what his Majesty the King had said as to the civilising effects of music upon the English nation and the way in which music brought all classes of the community in touch with each other. He would like to thank the pre- vious speakers for the strong and direct appeal they had made to the members of the club and to the solid phalanx of respectable artisans, with whom after all the destiny of the party really Jay. They had a great cause to fight for, and they must fight for it not only with energy but with prudence. (Hear, hear.) The proceedings; which had been of a very happy character, terminated with a vote of than to the Chairman, on the proposition of Mr. WORRA.LL.

SIR HOWARD VINCENT AT WARRINGTON.

TORIES TALK ON PROTECTION AND YELLOW LABOUR.

MR. PIERP3INT A" WII3LE-HO3ER."

Warrington Conservatives held a big domonstra- tion in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening, when Col. Sir Howard Vincent, the member for Central Sheffield delivered an address in support of the Protectionist proposals. Another of the principal speakers was Sir Henry Seton-Karr,M.P.(St.Helens) and Mr. Robert Pier- point, the borough member,again took the opportunity of declaring himself in favour of Mr, Chamberlain's policy. The hall was well-filled. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., presiding. Immediately behind him was a bevy of ladies, including Lady Greenall and Mrs. R. Pier- point, and the local leaders of Conservatism mustered in strong force. Amongst those on the platform were Alderman Sutton, Francomb, Smethurst, and Hutchin- son Councillor John Roberts, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Messrs. W. P. Rylands, J. E. Seeker, C. S. Burgess, W. E. Atkin, Vere B. Davies, E. Draper, C. Tinsley, J. Tinsley and Dr. Bower (Stretton). The CHAIRMAN announced the recsipt of letters of apology for non-attendance from Mr. J. Charlton Parr, Sir Peter Walker, Colonel Hall Walker, M.P., Colonel Pilkington, M.P.,and Mr. John Wuite. It was particu- larly disappointing to Mr. White not to be able to attend, as for the past twenty years be had been a con- vinced Fair Trader. _

At the outset, Sir HOWARD VINCENT referred to his associations with the late Sir Gilbert Greenall who, he said, was a model of an English gentleman—(applause) —a model of an English sportsman,and all that a repre- sentative of the British people should be. It had been the hope of many of the Chairman's friends that the time would come when he would pose his candidature before a constitnency—(applause)—and follow in his father's political footsteps,as he had followed in his foot- steps in many other directions. Continuing, the speaker said he came there as a business man, and as the repre- sentative of business men for the last twenty years in the House of Commons, to lay a business matter before the meeting. This was not to him a political matter. Attention was first directed to this question in the House of Commons in 1880 by Mr. Wheelhouse, but the real author of the Fair Trade movement was Mr. Ritchie, the late Chancellor of the Exchequer, who on the 24th of March, 1882, moved for the appointment of a Select Committee to inquire into the effects which the tariffs in force in foreign countries had upon British commerce. He did not agree with those who said we had made no progress in our condition during the last fifty years, for if it were so we should indeed be in a sorry condition. But we must sorrowfully admit there were many causes at work and many conditions of affairs in our midst which must cause thoughtful men food for serious and earnest reflection. Did he speak on behalf of his own constituents ? Yes. At the pre- sent time, and for many weeks past, upwards of 3,00 families in Sheffield had been dependent upon puslic Isubscription and philanthropy for one meal a day.

IWe had made progress during the last fifty years, but that progress had not been equal to the pro- gress of many other nations, and notably the ! United States of America, under an entirely j different system. The progress under our system of free imports which we had made during the first •thirty or forty years, down to 1870 or 1874, was largely 1 because we were comparatively at peace. The real industrial life of Europe within our own time dated from 1872 and the conclusion of peace between France and Germany. We had seen the unification of Germany and,after the great Civil War, the union of North and South America, when the States established Free Trade amongst themselves and a high tariff against the world. So soon was the effect felt that Mr. Ritchie within ten years had to direct the attention of the House of Commons to the state of affairs. The question was entirely one for the people and not for:the capitalist classes. It was true that wages had risen considerably—by reason of the action of protec- tionist associations, for what was a trade union but a protectionist association ? The distress of which we heard was not confined to Lancashire and Yorkshire. It existed in most large towns, and more partizularly in London. The "Labour Gazette " showed that among trade-unionists alone there were last month 36,767 unemployed, and if that was the condition of things among skilled workmen such as trade unionists, they knew that the number of unskilled workmen was sure to be far greater. There were last month 376,000 persons in receipt of out-door relief, and in the year 1903 261,36:3 of our own flesh and blood had to emigrate from England, and the great majority to a foreign country—where their labour would be protected—des- pairing of finding a livelihood in the old land. At the same time there came to London and other congested centres of population 70,000 to 80,000 of the scum of the earth, to fill our prisons and to drive English workmen out of their employment.—(Hear, hear.) Warrington was noted for its many industries, and the glass industry was once flourishing here. The iron and steel industries were amongst the principal trades. In what condition did they find the great majority of trades ? The fall in our exports in the ten years 1891-1900 compared with the previous ten years had been of no lessen amount than 137 million sterling. In the latter period there was a yearly average excess of imports over exports of 145 millions. He need not refer to the cotton industry. They all knew, far better than he did, the condition in which it was, and they would not be surprised to hear there was a fall of 60 millions in the ten years 1891 to 1900 in the export of cotton manufactures, compared with the exports of the pre- vious ten years. Their political opponents had ex- pressed opinions on the condition in which we found ourselves. Mr. Lloyd-George said Mr. Chamberlain was right" in so far as he said that things were not well in this country." It behoved men of all political parties, Sir H. Vincent said, to endeavour to find a remedy for this condition of affairs. The policy he ad- vocated was that of uniting our Empire with those who were anxious to treat with us, rather than give advantages and preferences in our own markets to foreigners who would not treat with us upon fair and equitable terms. Sir Howard Vincent quoted figures to show the increase of British exports to Canada since a preferential tariff was given In conclusion he alluded to the Chinese labour ques- tion. The United States of America, he said, found it absolutely necessary for the construction of the great rail-road system which had led to such extraordinary prosperity in the country, to employ Chinese labour. He would never consent to the uncontrolled immigra- tion of Chinese or any other labour into South Africa or any part of the dominion. It was absolutely necessary in the local opinion and in the opinion of impartial judges that labour which was at present unobtainable should be introduced in South Africa for the develop- ment of her natural resources. The Kaffir races had been aceustomed to do hard manual work below the ground, which the white man was not able to perform. They were now unwilling and unable to undertake the work in sufficient numbers and the conditions in the gold mines were such as to render it extremely difficult, if not impossible, for the white men to work there/They wanted to remove the oppression which existed in the white labour market in South Africa in order that the industries which were capable of givinc, full employ.- , ment might be developed. Therefore the local authori- ties, supported by the great mass of local opinion, had determined, under proper safeguards, to try the experi- ment of inviting for a limited period, the immigration of Chinese labourers. Under such conditions, the . majority of the House of Commons was of opinion that we ought not to run counter to local opinion. Mr. PIERPOINT, M.P., moved a vote of thanks to Sir Howard Vincent, and also "That this meeting is of opinion that the time has came when the fiscal policy of the United Kingdom must be reconsidered in order to secure reciprocal. treatment from foreign nations, ana the binding more closely together of the Empire by the establishment oi mutual preferences between the mother country and her children beyond the seas." The fiscal policy was, he said, above all, a question for ' the workingmen. There could be no doubt that the state of trade was not in a satisfactory condition. The tariffs in the United States, Germany, France, and other countries had been erected not simply to gain revenue, but, especially in some countries, absolutely with the almost-acknowledged ob ject of killing our markets and getting our trade. The Government's policy would provide us with a weapon with which to fight. As -it was now, the Government came before these other countries with its hands tied behind its back, unable to do anything but to negotiate—for it had nothing to offer; all its tariffs were gone. That was the policy of the Government'. For himself he looked to what he bel:eved to be a higher policy still. He was not looking entirely for the benefit of this kingdom—though that was the first that everyone who had a connection with English politics had to do —but he looked for the welfare and the unity of the empire. While supporting the policy of the Government to the fullest extent, he thought there was a man who was, as he had called himself, a " missionary of empire," whose proposals deserved consideration. He spoke, of course, of Mr, Chamberlain—(applause)—who offered them a system of taxation which would not, he was certain, affect by one farthing for the worse the food bill of any man in this country, but would give us an opportunity of giving the preference to the colonies which they asked for, and they vvould give us a preference in return. If, by a re-arrangement of our own tariffs, we could break down the tariff walls of foreign countries against us, he thought we would have done a great thing towards making foz the happiness of the great masses of the people of this country and the unity and strength of the empire. He would not be afraid to ask them to make a sacrifice for such an object, but he apprehended there would be no sacrifice whatever necessary. Mr. Chamberlain had not put forward any scheme which needed to be considered a hard, cut and dried scheme, but had merely sketched out a plan for consideration • and for working out into some definite plan which might ultimately benefit the kingdom and the empire. It was a great object, worthy of one of our greatest statesmen. He thought they wereright in thinking that Mr. Chamberlain was a man whom they would not be, at all events,foolish in follow- ing, because he was not only a statesman but a real business man. We were going to hold out before very long, he hoped, the familiar, friendly hand to our colonial brethren, in the hope that, *e would get not only a nation practic- ally self-supporting, but a nation capable of holding its own, whether it be in the field of commerce or in the field of warfare' against the whole world. The colonies must come to us; we must go to the colonies. We could not remain simply a little kingdom, but we tmust be the heart of a mighty empire. (Applause.) . __

s Sir H. SETON KARR, M.P., seconded. Mr. W. P. RYLANDS supported the resolution and spoke on the fiscal question from the point of view of the manufacturer. The position of English trade, he said, was indeed critical, owing to the unfair competi- tion to which we were subjected from American and German manufacturers. Among all the manufacturers. there was a unanimity of complaint that they had great difficulty in making both ends meet. It had been said that the fiscal question was a labour question, and he would endeavour to show that it was solely and only a labour question. The selling price of an article was made up of wages, rent, and the profit derived by the manu- facturer. Rents, of course, could not be done away with, and profits were things which the mannf tcturers • had for some time been accustomed to give up, and therefore could not be diminished in any way. If the manufacturers happened to be unable to make ends meet, there were two courses which they could adopt. They must either shut up shops and throw th%ir em- ployees out of work, or they must reduce the wages paid to the employees until they were in such a position as to be able to compete with th 6 foreigners. The throwing of men out of employment and the reduction in their wages necessarily meant a reduction in the standard of living, and although the standard of living in England was higher than it was in almost any country in the world, no one would suggest for a moment that it should be reduced. Therefore the two alternatives he had named were very undesirable. A third alterna- tive, which rested with the working classes, was that they should insist upon the Government instituting some measure of tariff reform. (Applause.) The resolution was put to the meeting and carried. In acknowledging the vote of thanks, Sir HOWARD VINCENT said he was very pleased so see Mr. Rylands who was a son of an old political opponent of his, on the platform, and to hear that he had the courage of his convictions. He could congratulate Mr. Pierpoint upon representing a constituency which was so active and intelligent. In that audience there would be many, perhaps, whose views did not coincide with those ex- pounded by the speakers, and he must thank them for the courtesy they had shown, and for their kindness in giving the speakers a fair hearing. (Applause.) The Chairman was thanked for presiding on the pro- position of Alderman SUTTON, seconded by Mr. J. FITLTON SMITH.

WARRINGTON 1111311L1.10 CORPS. Successful Concert hi the Parr Hall.

The funds of the War -ington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade will receive substantial assistance from the proceeds of a co2ceet held in the Parr Hall on Satarday evening. Tee concert had been arranged by the members of the corps, and the attendance, consider- ing the large nu über of concerts which have been held in thatown recantly, was highly sat'sfactory, and in itself an evideac3 of the appreciation of the Warring- ton public of the work of ' the corps wh;ca Captain Waving bas done so much to popalarise. The prograrnme was provided entirely by local artistes and the audience was so generous in its bestowal of favours as to recall each one of them. Mr. Jona R tbleitt, whose local reouiatioa was con- siderably enhanced by his performance in the opera given by the Musical Society, "Toa Yeomen of the Guard," was prime favourite, and received two recalls. He was not in tue best :orm, his voice being somewhat thick, but he gave such a vigorous interpretation of "Mona" as to demand a recall, and in complying he sang "Bid me to love." At a later step, he was agaia encored ;or his singing of "I'll sing thee songs of Araby." Mrs. C. Garton Stone sang "Corona- tion," with violin obligato by MrS..W. B. Roberts, but I she did herself greater justice in "It Bacio," for her fine rendering of which she was deservedly recalled. She complied with " Zutalar Zee," a song in which the :artiste bzoeght out all the piquant points of th 3 story. Miss Nellie Anderton, in her first elort sang Yosti's "Goodbye," in which an affectionate fare- well is given to the depart,ag summer. A critical audience might be disposed to consider thus song as a rttle unseasouable on a wet. February night, and more disposed to welcoine a refrain. to the cowing summer. Be tels as it may, Miss Anderion gave a very creditable rendering of the song and in responding to a recall she sang" Sweet Jane," winch, litt us hope, wi be a little sweeter—and dryer—than toe last. in the second half, Miss Aaderton sang" Serenade" (Schubert) and Awake "(Pelissiera),whica taken together form rather a long ii,em. Mr. J. B. Poole's bass voice•was heard to great advantage in the "Bedouin hove Soar," and on beinz encored he gave the ever-popular "The Village Blacksmith," with great effect. The four vocalists were afterwards heard together in a quartette in the well-known piece from the " Mikado"—"Brightly dawns our Wedding Day." Toe only instrumental item was a pianoforte solo by Mrs. W. B. Roberts, who played with such marked ability and refinement of expression, as to call forth loud applause from the audience and a recall. Mrs. Roberts also dis- charged the onerous duties of accompanist A feature of the evening's entertainment was the singing of the Apollo Male Voice Prize Choir. The choir' is unable to muster its fall strength on Saturday evenings, but no fewer than 36 were present on the platform and their singing was of a high quality. One of the prettiest pieces was the part song " Lovely Maiden," wnilst of the heavier choruses, tbe most adequately sung was "Spartan Heroes." In this dramatic chores, Mr. C. H.Hoult's tenor voice was keard to advantage in the solo part. Mr. T. Brough conducted the choir with his usual ability, and Mr. Trow accompanied two of the choruses on the piano. There were two encores. The hut-at:ter of the evening was supplied by Mr. Victor Roberts. Some of his humour was not of a very refined character, and the song "Woman," could hardly be conside,ed as com- plimentary to the fair sex. Nevertheless it is only fair to say that his ends were appreciated by the audience and he was recalled.

An exciting and tragic accident occurred on the river : Mersey at Runcorn on Saturday afternoon, by which a ! yeung man named Henry Parr, of Halton-rcad,Runcorn, lost his life. Parr was Mate to his father on the sail- ing flat Emily, belonging to Mr. Harrison, of Runcorn. The eraft, with a cargo of sulphur ore, was coming up the river on the flood tide when she was carried against one of the abutments of Runcorn Bridge. Young Parr was thrown into the water by the violence of the im- pact, and the flat received such damage that she imme- diately began to sink. The father got into a punt and went to his son's rescue, but just as he reached him the young man threw up his arms and sank. The flat drifted to the Ship Canal bank at Old Quay

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THE INEFFECTUAL BLACK LIST.

Warrington's on] y black-lister. John Wilfred Del oo ze' was charged at the Borough Police Court on Monday with obtaining drink from the Alexandra Vaults, Scot- land-road, on Saturday night, and also with making use of obscene language—P.C. Clough stated that be and P.C. Stewart found the prisoner in the taproom at the Alexandra Vaults, having a glass of beer. The landlady admitted having served him,but said se did not know he was on the black-list. On being spoken to, prisoner used bad language, and added that he would have more beer if ha liked. In the street he again used filthy langnago.—The Chief Constable said that when prisoner was placed on the black- list i January, 1903, a duplicate of his photograph was forwarded to every licencee. Since then, however, the licence of the Alexandra Vaults had changed hands. and the photograph was evidently not considered a transferable fixture. It was a scandalous shame that prisoner should go about trying to obtain drink. He VMS the only black-lister they had in War- rington; and the only black-Ester he (Mr. Talbot) wantesl.—The Mayor: I am aftaid we shall never makeangels with the black-list.—The Chief Constable ' said prisoner made his 31th appearance, and licensed ' victuallers needed protecting against him. The War- rington bench very properly discouraged the use of the I black-list, because he (Mr. Talbot) thought everybody wasagreed that itwas ineffectual. Prisoner was fined 295. and costs for obtaining drink and ss. and costs for using obscene language. "WA NTED " I LONDON.—SERIOTIS CHARGE. A young man named Charles Owen Trill appeared in the dock at the Wa.rrington Police Court on Thursday morning. The Chief Constable explained that on Wed- nesday afternoon the police received an intima;,ion by telegram from the Metropolitan Police, that a man of prisoner's name was wanted in London on a charge of obtaining £5O by means of false pretences. Sergeant Williamson and Dei,ective Barlow went in F:earch of the prisoner and found him at a house' in Price-street. He was taken to the police station and made no ddmor. In his possession friey found between 1,48 and .1;19 in cash Th 3 London police had now telegraphed that an officer from Scotland Yard would come to WerrinAton for the prisoner that day, and he (sr. Talbot) asked the magis- trates to make a formal order for prisoner to be handed over to the Metropolitan force.—The Bench made the necessary order.

VENTS OF THE WEEK.

In order to be nearer to the seat of war, the Mikado of Japan has moved his Court from Tokio to Kioto, which was the capital before the revolution of 1868. The ancient gold and silver bullion from the Imperial Treasury, and belonging to the old Daimios or feudal nobles, has been deposited with the Bank of Japan to form a reserve. Both Russia and Japan have instituted rigid press censorship. The little Power in this respect has followed the lead of her adversary, and certainly she must fight her big antagonist with the latter's own weapons, though, of course, the winning side is always first to relax these restrictions, having a preponder- ance of good news to tell. Assuming that Japan continues • as victoriously as she has begun, it follows that the best accounts will come through that source. This is the opinion of an official at the Eastern Telegraph Company in Moorgate Street. "Anything," he said, "coming from Japan or Korea must be genuine, even if it is cut about. It has been passed by the Government. The sway of the censorship ceases at Shanghai, where our lines begin. Correspondents contriving to get to Shanghai with their choicest stories will find an uninterrupted communication with their newspapers in London. \ There are two cables between Japan and Russia, and the ends have been sealed. I *should not be surprised if the Japanese have cut the cables, after dragging for them with grapnels. Their exact locality is easily found, being distinctly indicated An the charts."

A GREAT AMERICAN

No man has been more prominently or intimately connected with recent American polities than Senator Hanna, whose death occurred recently in his sixty-seventh year. He was, perhaps, the greatest figure of all the politlicians in the States, though never actually Presi- dent, and he was chief wire- puller to the Republican party. It was principally due to him that his great friend, the late President McKinley, was elected to 'the head office of the Republic, and it has several times been rumoured during the last year that he might even stand for the Presidency himself at the THE LATE SENATOR HANNA. expiration of

Pre-sidentßoose- velt's term. Death, however, has settled that question and removed his strong per- sonality from the sphere of American politics. Born in 1837, he was educated ,at the Western Reserve University and entered upon his business career as partner in a wholesale grocery house, but at the age of thirty he blossomed out upon a far wider scale, and in due course became president of several large companies and corporations, including M. Hanna and Co., coal merchants, the National Union Bank, the Cleveland City Railway Company, 'and the Chapin Mining Company, growing to be one of the wealthiest' men in the States. Then he took up politics, where, as remarked above, he became the chief force in the Republican party, eeater even than the President himself, who owed his very nomination to him. He entered the U.S. Senate in 1897 upon the nomination of Governor Bushnell, and has since been twice re-elected. His death will bring about many alterations in the party organisation of thy Republicans.

A POPULAR PEER.

There were few more popular men than Lord Alington, who died at the end of last week, and he was popular with all ranks from the King downwards. In fact, he was the first of His Majesty's subjects to have the honour of entertaining him after his Accession. Though seventy-nine years of age, until the last year or so, Lord Alington was a hale, hearty man, spare in figure and upright in carriage, young beyond his years. Indeed it was only in 1892 that he married as his second wife Mr. " Bunny " Leigh's sister in the same year that his beautiful daughter married ,Lord Cadogan's eldest son and became Lady Chelsea. Not only was he

THE LATE LORD ALINGTON AND HIS SUCCESSOR.

a notable figure in Society and on a race- .course, but he w,as a most generous landlord, and everyone upon his estate at Crichel, in Dorset, is deeply in mourning for his loss. Crichel is one of the. show places of England and is famous for its " white farm," which was the first Lady Alington's hobby and has always been preserved. One notable point about the estate is that Lord Alington, who• was very fond of birds, would not alTow a gun to be fired! But the turf was his chief hobby, and ,to it he was passionately devoted from his boyhood, when he used to run horses under all sorts of assumed names to avoid the parential anger. His open connection with racing dates from 1849, and his famous partnership with Sir Frederic lohnstone, "the Old Firm," as they were called, began in 1868. Two Derbies fell to them, in addition to many famous successes, and they trained for many years with John Porter at Kingsclere. Lord Alington is succeeded by his son, Mr. Humphrey Sturt, M.P. for East Dorset and this will entail a bye- election.

LATEST HIPPODROME SENSATION.

The latest sensation at the London Hippo- drome is described in the programme as The Donatellis—The Living Cycling Arena." There are two Donatellis. Before they per- form their great feat, they do other surpris- ing things with a bicycle. One Donatelli fixes a pair of horizontal bars to his shoul- ders, and, thus equipped, rides his bicycle. While he is going round the ring, the other Donatelli jumps up and gives a display of his skill on the horizontal bars. For the performance of the great feat the more passive of the two Donatellis prepares him- self by putting a light frame on his shoulders. Four short rods protrude above The shoulders and these rods fit into four sockets under the

===== 0002601_19040227_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LIVING CYCLE ARENA.

cycling track that Donatelli is to bear. The track is carried, into the ring by some dozen men, and it certainly appears to be a very weighty piece of apparatus. When it is properly fixed on the shoulders of one Donatelli, the other one leaps up on to his bicycle in the track. Then someone shouts "Go," the audience are very quiet, the band stops, and the rider Donatelli flies round and round the track on his bicycle. He goes so fast that everyone's attention is drawn to him, and people forget that the track is being held all the time on the shoulders of one man. However, when the performance is finished the audience realise its danger and difficulty, and applaud ac- cordingly. It is certainly a very wonderful feat, and one which we believe has never before been attempted.

THE NEW MID-HERTS MEMBER

It is unlikely that, when Mr. Vicary Gibbs acted as agent for the British Government in the acquirement of the Chili warships re- cently on sale, he 'realised that it would cost him his seat in Parliament. However, having made a personal profit upon the transaction, he was forced by the rules of the House to resign and offer him- self to his con- stituents for re- election, and St. Albans, despite its Conservative traditions and cathedral asso- ciations, e- jected him in favour •of a Radical and a Nonconformist. This was due to no personal motives, but to a desire for a completechange and the election MR. BAMFORD SLACK, Mr was fought mainly upon the question of the Education Bill. The new member for Mid-Herts is Mr. John Bamford Slack, the well-known Queen Vic- toria Street solicitor, who was born at Rip- ley in Derbyshire on July 11th, 1857, so he is still quite a young man politically. He practised as a solicitor for some years in the Midlands and first entered politics by seeking election for the borough of Ilkeston on the Derbyshire County Council. He was always closely connected with all Methodist and temperance movements, and, upon coming to London in 1889, Mr. Bamford Slack not only continued his practice as a lawyer, but also kept up a close connection with both these causes. He is, also, a Fellow of the Royal Geographical Society, the Zoological Society, and the Bioliographical Society, and he is considered an authority on old books. His hobby is mountaineering, and he was edu- cated at London University, of which he is a B.A.

A RYE RELIC.

From Rye in Sussex comes an interesting appeal for subscriptions. 'lt emanates from the Vicar and Churchwardens of that cele- brated Cinque Port, which was in its day the " Spithead " of the country, affording harbour to as many as a hundred ships at a time. The appeal is directed especially to archologists, antiquarians, and all interested in Church work, and its object is to raise 1,20.0 to buy up the old monastery chapel for the use of the church. It stands on Conduit Hill, which leads out of the High Street, and although a very quaint and in- teresting old building, it is, however, com- paratively modern in relation to other parts of the town, as it only dates from Henry VIII.'s, reign, whilst they go back two and three hundred years further still. It was originally the home of the Augustine Friars until th d tablishment, but in 1572 it

===== 0002601_19040227_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
LE OLD RYE MONASTERY

saw a complete change, when it was ,taken possession of by Huguenots who fled after the massacre of St. Bartholomew's Day, and were reinforced in the following century by others exiled, by the Edict of Nantes. • Since then it has had many vi.cissitudes, and been put to many uses. This erstwhile monastery chapel has served as barracks for the soldiers quartered in the town, a theatre, a malt- house, a storehouse, a wool warehouse, and, last of all. as a " Salvation. y " meeting house. Thus, from its strange and romantic history, religious and secular, its restoration and final acquirements should appeal to all classes specially approached.

GOOD TEMPLARY.

Annual District Lodge Session.

The annual session of the South-West Lancashire Dis- trict Lodge of the 1.0.G.T., was held on Saturday in the Wesley Hall,Soho-street, Liverpool. Bro. the)Rev. J. A. Lee, D.C.T. (Liverpool) presided over a large attendance of representatives and visitors from the various parts of the district, which extends from South- port to Warrington, and from Liverpool to Leigh. A large number of distinguished visitors were received from West Cheshire, India, North Stafford, and Malta. Thirty-eight candidates were admitted into D.L. degree. The reports of the D,C.T. and the D. Sec. (Bro. Morris Jones, Liverpool) showed that although there was a decrease on the year's working, there was an increase of 10 on the last quarter. Activity was shown in the various lodges. The cause of the decrease in some of the lodges seemed to be the depression in trade. The Earlestown, Warrington and other country lodges seemed to be suffering from this cause, especially Warrington where the lodge although reporting a decrease of 12 on the quarter and 20 on the year, never worked harder in their existence. (Hear. bear-) The D. Sec. urged that more public work should be taken part in. He said the Executive would arrange during the coming year, a good series of demonstrations and church parades, weather permitting. (Laughter.) The report of Bro. G. B. Walker, D. Treasurer showed a balance in hand of £8 is. 9d. Bro.E. Newman,D.S.J.T.(Earlestown)gave an interest jag report of the work among the juveniles and appealed for more help from the adult members of the order. There was a decrease of members on the quarter. Bro. G. S. Schofield, D.E.S., reported on the electoral work of the district which was quite up to the usual standard of efficiency. The report contained a very strong anti-compensation resolution which was enthusi- astically received. .

wirrhe reports of the• Foreign Correspondent,Registrarlof Templar Institute, Grand Lodrzs collector and Orphan- age collector showed interest being taken in all these departments except the Templar Institute. The reports were all accepted with thanks to the officers.

A petition was read from the Tvldesley lodge, asking to be transferred to the South East Lancashire Dis- trict. This was granted subject to the approval of the Grand Lodge.

The eleciiou of the representatives to Grand Lodge resulted in the election of Bros. J. A. Lee. G. B. Walker, R. D. Pettarson (Liverpool), E. Newman (Earlestown), and Sister Duncan (Liverpool). The result of the election of officers was as follows: —D.C.T., Bro. the Rev. J. A. Lee (Liverpool); D. Coun- cillor, Bro. R. D. Petterson (Liverpool); D.S.J.T., Bro. E. Newman (Eawlestown) ; D E.S. Bro. Rycroft (Liverpool); D. Sec., Bro. Morris Jones (Liverpool) ; D. ',Treas., Bro. G. B. Walker (Liverpool) ; D.V.T., Sister Rycroft (Liverpool); D. Chaplain, Bro. Oliver (Liverpool); D. (West Cheshire) and the session closed at about 10 o'clock, having lasted from 2 o'clock with an hour's interval for tea.

THE " SANIT AS COMPANY, LIMITED.

The annual general meeting of the " Sanitas "Com- pany, Limited, was held on Wednesday, February 24th, at their Limehouse Works, under the presidency of Mr. C. 'P. Kingzett, F.1.C., F.C.S. The Chairman congratu- lated the shareholders upon the continued prosperity'of the business, notwithstanding the generally depressed state of trade tbrougboat the country and the bad weather which bad been very adverse to their interests. In particular, be directed attention to the "Pine- Oxygen" Treatment of Consumption and Lung and Throat Affections which had been elaborated by the Company' and he ant;eipated a growing trade in the sale of "Sanitas "Oil and" Sanitas " Fumigators and Inhalers, which appliances are associated with that treatment. It was pointed out that this treatment of consumption is inexpensive and is within the reach of the very poor as well as the rich, while it does not necessitate residenee abroad and obviates the rigours of the so-called open-air cure. Reference was also made to a new Antiseptic Dentifrice the Comppy had recently placed on the market known as Zanol." Turning to the accounts of the Company, after making provision for bad and doubtful debts, repairs to plant and machinery, and advertising, a final dividend and bonus of 4 per cent. was declared (making a total dis- tribution of 7i per cent. for the year), the sum of .£5OO was carried to depreciation, £1,500 to reserve, and a balance of £2,227 forward to 1904.

, THE SETTLEMENT OF WESTERN CANADA. The favourable reports from persons who have settled and are farming in the Canadian West have assisted in removing questions of doubt as to the fertility and productiveness of the land upon which the British people are invited to locate. These, together with the unexampled success which has followed the efforts of the farmers of that country, should, and widoubtedly will, result in a vast number of those who feel that their conditions are not as satisfactory as they should be—selecting Canada as offering the best opportunities for future success in life. The Government of Canada continues its offer of a free grant of 160 acres of land to every man over 18 years of age. Over 32,000 persons, representing considerably over 100,000 people, have taken advantage of this offer of the Government within the past 12 months, representing an area settled upon of 5,000,000 acres of hurl. Although the Government has already made free grants to actual settlers to the extent of about 12,000,000 acres, there is yet an area many times as great, and of soil as rich and productive, ! still to be had. The land is simply in its virgin state ' waiting caltivation. Those who contemplate taking advantage of these free lands, either immediately on their arrival in Canada or after a year or so spent on a ifarm gaining practical experience before going out,‘are advised taplace themselves in communication with Mr. W. T. R. Preston, Canadian Emigration Mee, 11 and 11 Charing Cross, London, and there obtain reliable in- formation as to the best method to follow. 46

COTTON VERSUS SUGAR GROWING.

Mr. George Hughes, a well known West Indian chemist has introduced a process by means of which the pith of the sugar cane is converted into a meal containing 75 per cent. of digestible matter, which has the remarkable property of being able to absorb over five times its own weight of Molasses. It can then be shipped in bags to any part of the world as a cattle food, and mixes readily with the other animal foods. This "IVlolascut " as it is called has proved a great success in Argentine where it has been in use for come time past. It improves the digestion weight and general condition of the animals, and its manufacture has created a new industry ia the West Indies and other sugar growing countries.
