FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

We may rest assured that the rulers the world of dress are now busy evolving novelties with which to relieve the recent monotony of the modes, and that in a few weeks our eyes will be gladdened by the debut of the early spring fashions. In the mean- time, however, we must rest content with things as they are; and, indeed, we have little cause for complaint when we review the present style of dress which, although rather elaborate, is exceedingly becoming and artis- tic. The prudent-minded of us will seize the opportunity offered by the best firms of reduced terms during the silly season and. trusting that Madame La Mode will not in- dulge in anything very startling in the next few months, will order a tweed or serge gown which, if simply made but perfect as to cut and fit, will be found invaluable throughout the ensuing months. I always counsel my friends, and especially those whose dress allowance is limited, to deny themselves the many tempting offers of soiled model gowns greatly reduced in price that are on view at

A well-fitted navy blue serge or black cloth costume of not too heavy a make ought with a little renovating and pressing to stand wear for- a whole year. The neat coat sketched is suited also for wear off and on for the next season. It is made of smooth-surfaced but non-spotting and unshrinkable navy blue cloth,, with the capes and stole ends piped with scarlet silk. Many of the seams are also treated in the same manner, and a further finish is given by the ornamental buttons.. This style is smart and simple and suited for a girl of six upwards. The hat which completes the costume is of navy blue felt with a red velvet bow under the brim and a long ostrich feather curling round the crown and over, the brim at the back. A DINNER BLOUSEJ

which the coat would look very well, for, at present, brown is perhaps the most fashion- able colour. Some of the most fashionable shades might almost be termed bronze, and some of them merge into curious greens, which are certainly novel if not particularly pretty. A contrasting note of bright colour is generally introduced on these dull-toned toilettes; and the most popular as well as the most becoming is cherry, which, so it is whis- pered, will increase in favour as the spring advances. It is certainly a most pleasing colour, and is equally becoming to the fair and the dark complexioned. On the Continent those who dine out do not affect the decollete toilettes worn in this country, but prefer a style of dress which though equally smart exposes the wearer less to the disastrous consequences of a chill. A pretty blouse worn at a dinner in one of the best restaurants by an equally charming Parisienne was made in the style shown in my. second sketch. The material employed was cerise pleated crepe de chine over silk, trimmed with bands of handsome silk embroidery. RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A Mortuary Require 4

p66A

The monthly meeting of this Council was held a Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. G. Pilling (vice chairman) presiding. There were also present : Messrs. C. E. Linalter, T. Wright, J. Moseley Williams, A. G. Guest, J. Mounfteld, T. Baxter: C. Roylance, J. Spruce, J. L Jones, J. B. Chadwick, T. Antwiss, J. Edwards, T. Darlington, A. Greenway, J. Nickson, with Dr. Adams (medical officer of ihealth), Messrs. W. E. Hough (deputy clerk), W. Farrington (inspector of nuisances), .1 .Swinton and W. Diggle (surveyors).

===== 0002601_19040227_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
WESTON SEWERAGE SCHEME

It was reported that at a meeting of the Weston Parochial Committee, Mr. J. Diggle, the engineer for the proposed sewerage scheme dealt fully with the plans, and it was . resolved that a recommendation he made to the District Council that the scheme as prepared be adopted, and that appli- cation be made to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £2,500 for the work. It was further resolved that the Manchester Ship Canal Company be required to provide the Rural Council with a perpetual right to discharge sewage from the parish of Weston through the culvert at Folly Brook or to construct and provide such outfall as may be necessary for the dis- charge of such sewage into the river Mersey. The Clerk reported that he had received a tender for an installation oflelectric light in the parish for street light ingipurposes. The matter was referred to the Lighting Committee. The minutes were confirmed, and the Council agreed to make application to the Local Government Board for sanction to borrow £2,500. STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION. The Deputy-clerk produced an order from the Cheshire County Council increasing the representation of Stockton Heath on the District Council from one to two members. The order comes into force after April 15th. A MORTUARY WANTED AT WESTON.

-- Mr. R. Dobson, coroner, wrote stating that he had recently held an inquest at Weston Point on a body on which a post-mortem examination had to be made. The body had to be removed to a stable of a public house. Dr. McDougall, who conducted the examin- tigii pointed out in Court the absence of proper pro- viVion for dead bodies. A mortuary properly fitted for the holding of dead bodies was a matter of urgent necessity. Mr. Wright said the question came before the Coun- cil two years ago, and it was considered rather unfair that Weston alone should be put to the expense of pro- viding a mortuary. It was suggested that a boat should be used for the purpose so that it would be available when bodies were recovered at various places in the district. The Weaver Navigation Trustees were ap- proached, but the matter fell to the ground. Mr. Clarke : Is it the duty of the District Council to provide a mortuary? The Deputy-Clerk: It is quite optional with the Council whether they provide a mortuary or not, but they have power to do so. Mr. Linaker said there had been many cases of drown- ing in the Weaver at Frodsham Bridge and the same difficulty existed there as at Weston. He was in favour of a floating mortuary which would serve the whole district.

The Deputy-Clerk said he understood the difficulty was in regard to the mooring of the boat. Mr. Linaltibr : There is plenty of room in the Weaver rear Frodsham Bridge. It was decided to again approach the Weaver Trustees forwarding them a copy of the Coroner's letter and a report of the inquest.

===== 0002601_19040227_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. Adams reported that one case of scarlet fever at Grappenhall and another at Stockton Heath had been notified Grappenhall schoo., which had recently been closed owing to an outbreak of measles, had now re-opened, but, as he was informed that the attend- ance was very meagre, he had requested that further information should be supplied. He received a tele- graphio intimation that a case of smallpox had been discovered at Helsby. but on examination he found that the patient was suffering from chicken-pox. Several ether cases of chicken-pox had been notified. The report was adopted. ELECTRICITY POWER AND GAS BILL.

It was resolved that the seal of the Council be affixed to a resolution supporting the Cheshire Electricity Power and Gas Bill, the object of which is to provide a cheap supply of. electricity and power gas for the purpose of heating and power.

===== 0002601_19040227_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====

The seal of the Council was affixed to agreements with the Liverpool Corporation for the supply of water to Dutton and Preston-o'-th'-Hill and to twenty-six house's at Norley.

BRIBERY AT ELECTIONS.

Offender Sent to Gaol at Grimsby.

Thomas Melvin, fish merchant's manager; Henry Brown, club steward; and William Andrew s, tobacco- nist, were indicted at Lincoln Assizes on Monday for bribery at the Grimsby municipal bye-election on Sep- tember 22nd last. -

Mr. Bounor prosecuted on behalf of the Public Prosecutor, and Mr. Walker defended. The case against Melvin was taken first. Mr. Bonnor stating that defendant was alleged to have given several voters one shilling each to vote for Pettit, who was duly returned by a large majority, with the result that the second candidate, Mr. C. W. Barker, instituted a petition for bribery, which was heard at 'Grimsby in December and resulted in Pettit being unseated. •. A number of witnesses gave evidence as to the receipt of a shilling from Melvin, but all of them displayed a unanimity in being certain that Melvin never mentioned the name of any particular candidate. Some amuse- ment was caused by the fact that one of the witnesses was an elderly widow named Otley, her husband being an extown councillor. She bad received a shilling, but was positive Pettit's name was never suggested to her, though she knew perfectly well whom Melvin was working for. She went with "other ladies" in the trap to the polling booth. The charges against Brown and Andrews, though less in the number of cases, were substantially of a similar nature, Brown having, it was alleged, paid a shilling to voters at the Social club, and Andrews a like amount to voters whilst conveying them to the polling booth. Melvin having been found guilty,Mr. Justice Bucknill said that to line him would be a ,farce. The charges 'mei') extremely serious, and as the judges desired to put a stop to bribery at elections Melvin would be sentenced to one month in the second division as a warning that future offenders, if found out, might expect to be more severely dealt with. ' Brown and Andrews were discharged, the judge sug- gesting that no evidenceshould be offered.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR. I NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday.

Sir Howard Vincent, and Chinese Labour.

Sir,—Just at the point in his speech, where he intro- duced the subject of Chinese labour in South Africa, Sir Howard did me the great honbur, this (Thursday) evening, of pickingup from the Chairman's table a copy of my little pamphlet, entitled "One-sided Free-Trade v. Protection,' and, with very kindly grace, he gave explanatory details to show that I had failed to grasp some of tire factors in this problem of "yellow labour." It has occurred to me that many among the audience might perchance infer—from Sir Howard's introductory observations—that I had touched upon this question of Chinese labour, ha course of the pamphlet (a reprint of one out of a series of eight fiscal letters written by me, a while ago), so I 'am burning a little midnight oil to make it quite clear that I have—until now—never pub- lished a line about this importation of Chinese into South Africa. The fiscal articles were written some months ago, before Mr. Chamberlain's campaign, and, of course,prior to this subjeot of Chinese labour coming forward as a public question. As a matter of fact, I was speaking and writing as a Fair-trader, over twenty years ago, and my little pamphlet, and my other recent contributions to the press, were chiefly reproductions of material I had been accumulating during a long period of time. As some of your readers may be interested in hearing how it happened Sir Howard prefaced his remarks, as to Chinese labour by an allusion to my views on the subject, I will briefly outline the circumstances Those who this evening heard the gallant Colonel eordially inviting correspondence from any one who was in doubt upon points connected with the great public questions of the moment, will understand that Sir Howard,like most other really great men, is very approachable, and,knowing his reputation on this point, and also having been for many years a warm admirer of his frank outspokenness, I took the liberty of writing him a few days ago, submitting views strongly opposed to the introduction, into South Africa, of what I then considered as" slave labour." There were whispers iu the town,that Sir Howard was to be " tackled " upon this awkward question, so, pos- sibly, my letter may have done a useful public service, for he entered—more fully than perhaps he otherwise might have done—into !the very heart of the problem tonight, giving us practical details which he had acquired on the spot. Whether my letter had anything to do with it or not, we must give Sir Howard great credit for his generalship in" grasping the nettle,' and, by anticipatory tactics, weakening any opposition party that might have come to Parr Hall,in the hope of creat- ing a scene in regard to a matter af South African policy upon which, undoubtedly, there is just now much public comment—md probably among Conservatives quite as much as among Liberals. Sir Howard -Vincent has won the grand and good name of being one of the Bayards of British politics—a knight without fear and without reproach, chivalrous, truthful, kindly a keen, bat perfectly fair, lighter ; therefdre, when he assures us, from his own pertonal knowledge (and he has visited, in turn, every British possession, and studied most of our Imperial problems on the spot) that Chinese labour is, for a time at any rate, an absolutely indisponsable condition of South African progress, I think we may safely dismiss any doubts or fears we may have individually held, as to the wisdom of the policy. Yours, &c., H. P. HUGHES. Fairfield road, Stockton Heath, 25th February, 1901.

CRIPPLED GIRLS' FLORAL

EXHIBITION.

A Deserving Work.

The Parr Hall will be the scene of an interesting floral exhibition next week, and Warringtonians will be afforded an opportunity of aiding a most deserving institution—the Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission. Briefly stated, the object of the mission is to train destitute, crippled girls' who think themselves of no use and helpless, to become self-supporting. Girls are received from all parts of the country, and the very beautiful flowers which will be shown at the exhibition are the work of the inmates of the Industrial Homes. The founder and superintendent of the institution is Mr. John A. Groom, who has entered into the work heart and soul. Whilst yet in his teens, he became convinced that something ought to be done for the slum children of London, and especially for those crippbd and afflicted children who were perambulated in the streets by cruel guardians for the purpose of securing alms. At length he waited on the Earl of Shaftesbury and laid the whole matter before him. "Gather as many of these poor creatures together as you can," said the Earl " Get all the girls and children. I will come and see them, and talk with them,and by God's blessing will help them." Mr. Groom did as requested and the Earl listened to the children's tales of sorrow, heroism, suffering and crime. Visibly affected,the Earl said,'Ve will help you if you yourselves will help us to help you." This was the -beginning of the Watercress and Flbwer Girls' Christian Mission' and from that time tne work has rapidly increased. At the homes, the mission workers house the crippled and friendless children, and teach them to make artificial flowers. Thus the girls become not only independent, but receive as pocket money any earnings which remain after paying the weekly cost of board and lodgings. A department has been opened for renovating flowers which would otherwise be dis- carded by ladies as useless. The work is deserving of every support from all classes of the community,and we hope the exhibition, which will be opened on Monday afternoon by the Mayor and Mayoress and continue until Saturday evening, will be a great success. A con- tingent of crippled girls will be in attendance, and visitors to the hall will then be able to see the ac,tual process of artificial horticulture. Music will be supplied by Miss Polly Houghton (soprano), Mr. L. Pownall (bass), Mr. Tom Boothroyd (violinist), Mr. A. Trow (pianist), and the members of Mr. Tom Boothroyd's orchestra. There will be no charge for admission.

BIBLE SOCIETY CENTENARY. The Celebration on March 6th.

March 6th next is Bible Sunday, and this year the hundredth anniversary of the British and Foreign Bible Society will be celebrated on that day. We commend the following letter from the local secretaries to the attention of our readers. Sir,—May we be permitted to draw the attention of your readers to the advertisement in this paper of the approaching celebration in this district of the hundredth anniversary of the British and Foreign Bible Society. It will be noted that in a large number of churches and chapels arrangements have been signi- fied of intentions to take part in the celebration on Bible Sunday which is March 6th. We trust that it may yet be possible for others to make similar arrangements, and if they will kindly let us know, their services can be inserted in next Saturday's paper. Your readers generally may be interested in knowing that this great Christian Institution during the century of its existence has been the means of circulating 185,000,000 copies of the Bible. It has issued the Bible in 370 languages. It has 7,875 auxiliaries and branches. Its expenditure in 1903, for the year was £254,000. Its entire outlay during its existence has been over £14,000,000. The Society sells copies of Bible, New Testament, and separate books, at nominal prices which the poorest can pay. It has issued 8,000,000 copies of the Penny New Testament, and issues the whole Bible for sixpence. It is desired by this Centenary Celebration to raise a fund of a quarter of a million sterling to meet important claims from all quarters of the world. Further it may be stated that the Centenary Sunday mill be observed all over the United King- dom and in various parts of the Christian world. Their Majesties the King and Queen have intimated their intention of being present on that day in St. Paul's Cathedral, London:when the sermon will be preached by the Archbishop of Canterbury. The Lord Mayor and Sheriffs of London will attend the service in state. Corporations of nearly all the Cities and Towns of this country will attend similar services, and we trust that this fact may find recognition by the Mayor and Corporation of Warrington. We invite co-operation on this great occasion on the part of Christians of all Denominations who desire the world wide circulation of Holy Scrip- ture.—Yours faithfully, E. C. E. CARLETON, JOHN YONGE, S. P. SILCOCK. (Secretaries). February 25th, 1901.

Two girls, who had a skipping rope twisted round their waists, ran from some children with whom they had been playing in Gooch-street, Birmingham, .on Thursday night. In crossing the road one fell in front of a waggon, dragging the other down with her. One child was killed on the spot. The other had both legs broken.

At the funeral at Dover, on Thursday, of Mr. Thomas Longley, the King's heaviest subject, it took ten strong men to carry the coffin, which was of such dimensions that it had to be let through the window, the door being too narrow.

Rugby Football.

By " &Mist."

Warricgton failed to win again last Saturday, but they accomplished a good performance in drawing at Swinton under circumstances the reverse of favourt able. It may not be quite true but Warrington are- generally regarded by their supporters as a fair- weather team. Certainly a dry day and firm turf are more to Warrington's advantage than they would be to some teams. But on Saturday though rain fell incess- antly and the ground was inches deep in mud the wirepullers” gave a display worthy of their palnii- est days. In fact they only need to combine as well every week to secure honours of the sort which generally contrive to elude their grasp. Swinton were outplayed at every point, and nothing but luck pure and unadulterated prevented them being beaten by a handsome margin. The form of the visitors quite surprised the crowd,whoprobably anticipated a couple of points at Warrington's expense. Instead they were glad when the final arrived and the Blues were safely in possession of one point.

Some referees would have been inclined to declare the match off, so bad were the conditions for serious football, but Mr. Priestley evidently preferred to get the fixture over. Nobody anticipated anything but a scramble, yet strange to say, the game was really well worth watching. There was more open football by far than one sees in many an encounter where there is no excuse for keeping the ball close. Warrington, in particular, did some excellent passing and running only to fail at the critical moment, often by ill-luck. Twice for instance a pass forward resulted in the scorer being brought back, while Harris was checked by Chorley no less than three times right at the corner when it seemed a guinea to a gooseberry on him getting in. The impossibility of doubling on the miry surface probably had a lot to do with Harris's failure to get through, but I should never have believed that he could have been so effectually checked on the verge of the line by any full-back three times in succession had I not been there to see.

For once in a way the Warrington forwards dis- tinguished themselves. Though the Blues appeared to. have the bigger men Warrington always had a slight pull in the scrummage, and this resulted in the backs being kept going. Both Hockenhall and Bate lost no time in slinging the heavy ball to 'the three- quarters once they saw that the quartette could easily out-manouvre the opposition. Bate sent some particularly good passes—necessarily slow on this occasion—while Hockenhall seemed to be everywhere. His defensive work and spoiling tactics were of great service to Warrington. Fish was perhaps the worst of the Warrington backs, for he seemed quite out of his element in the mud and moisture which abounded. Sprinting was out of the question and more than once he had to resort to a punt owing to the shocking foot-hold. Still he would have won the match in the first five minutes had not Isherwood mulled a lovely pass near the line. The captain did good work afterwards which atoned somewhat for the blunder. Both full backs shone on the defence and had a good deal to do with the score- sheet remaining blank to the end.

Dave Traynpr made a re-appearance in first-class football, but I could detect no indications of his ever being much use to the Blues' third line. He was too slow to be effective, and on Saturday when the ball came his way, he generally found that Isherwood or Dickenson came with it. Opposite Harris was a lengthy wing man known to fame (?) as Martin Burrows, and a regular muff he proved. Possibly there is enough football in him to make a scrummager, but nothing else. He was outclassed by his opponent, who proved a thorn in the side of the Blues.

My suggestions last week with regard to experi- ments in the WA division of the first fifteen have aroused considerable interest, and I am indebted to a number of correspondents for kindly giving me their views. Counter-suggestions are volunteered in some cases, and altogether there is plenty of matter for careful consideration, the importance of which is en- hanced by the near approach of the cup-ties. It is necessary that the Warrington attack should be strengthened, and perhaps some of the suggestions given below may help the Clnanaittee in effecting the desired improvement.

" WELL-VASH ER " writes:—" The football notes of 'Ovalist,' at all times interesting,were especially SO in last Saturday's issue by reason of the daring proposals therein contained. Those proposals were to play Fish centre three-quarter and Thomas half- back. In my opinion bothpropositions are worthy of serious consideration. Warrington has this season played six half-backs and yet the position is unsatisfactorily filled. Thomas has displayed skill speed, and resource in open field work which entitle bim to a trial at half-back. Salford, when Lomas was admittedly the finest half-back in Lancashire, did not hesitate to play him centre threequarter. So let Warrington Committee 'take their courage ml both hands' and try Thomas at half. At the worst it is but one more to the list of experiments, but it may be the strengthening of Warrington's weakest spot. Changes in the third line might not be necessary with capable half backs. Still the change of Fish to the centre position is worthy of trial. It has been intensely irritating to spectators to see a player with the abilities of Fish simply unable to get the ball. Jack is one who can make his own play and granted he handles the ball oftener there is more than an even chance the scoring would be larger. As one anxious for the welfare of the W.F.C., I should like to see your proposals acted upon."

" SPECTATOR " says :—" I was very pleased to see your remarks re the changes in the Warrington team. I think it is time our genial captain had a rest to get in trim for the cup ties. With regard to putting Thomas in the half-back position I partly agree with you, but it would certainly lessen the weight of the pack. The Committee have not been so wide awake as they might have been in the search for players. or we would not have had so many defeats in Yorkshire.'

" MEMBER " writes: "I think your suggestion in last week's Examiner is a very good one, and the asking for opinions and ideas is one that should meet with a good response, as the Warrington fol- lowers are most loyal to the Primrose and Blue,and the Football Committee are, I believe, ready to consider any ideas that may be put forward in the right spirit. An experiment I should like to see made is to put Isherwood at half with Davies, bring Fish centre and Kenyon wing. By this 'you have a treble advantage : (1) You still maintain the order of playing,that is Isherwood would play next to Fish. (2) The introducing of our be.st reserve without impairing the defence. (3) You put someone at the base who is not without initiative which is undoubtedly what is required in the War- rington team. I wish the movement so admirably suggested every success."

"G. W. " writes ;—" I quite 'agree with what you say in reference to the three-quarters, but so far as half backs are concerned I am not so sure. Thomas would no doubt make a good half back, but who is to take his place in the forwards? We are short of clever men there as it is. I should also like to comment on the way the reserve half backs are chosen. Half backs and three-quarters are changed about, and played first in one position and then the other, which ram sure is bad policy. I refer more particularlyto flockenhall and Mahoney Now, again, what about our scoring ability or rather lack of it, when pressing on our opponents' line? The fault here, to my mind, lies in not getting the ball out to the wings quickly enough. I think if the three-quarters would stand well back, and the half back pass out quickly without attempting to move a yard, our wing men would score oftener in these cases ; other teams can do it. What about the Oldham match when we were pressing continu- ally, and getting possession, and still could not score ! It is all very well for the half back to try to beat his own man, but give the wing man the ball, and plenty of room to beat his man, instead of running him on the touch line first, and I am con- vin9ed we shall get more fun for our money."

Warrington will have an opportunity of avenging one of this season's defeats to-day, when Hull Kingston Rovers are due at Wilderspool. The Rovers' ground was visited an October 31st, when the home team were going ahead strongly, and Warrington were un- fortunate in sustaining defeat by a penalty goal to nothing. Since those days the Rovers have come down the ladder of fame somewhat and now hold a position inferior to Warrington in the League table. They should be conquered by a handsome margin to-day if current form is anything to go by.

The home team will be the same as at Swinton, with the exception that Fairhurst, the old original "Silver," comes into the forwards in place of Lunt, while Davies takes Bate's position behind the scrum. He will be at liberty again after to-day. The team therefore is: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isher- wood, Dickenson, and Harris, three-quarters; Bate and Hockenhall, half-backs ;Boardman, Thomas, Cook, Edmondson, Fairhurst, Naylor, Jolley and Harmer, forwards.

At a meeting of the Lancashire County Union Com- mittee on Tuesday evening the following players were awarded county caps :—F. Baxter (Rochdale Hornets), A. S. Boardman (Warrington), and H. Buckler (Salford). It wits resolved that championship medals be presented to the president and the whole of the players who had taken part in the championship tourney.

A Northern Union County match was played at Birkenhead on Wednesday between Cheshire and Durham and Northumberland. The teams proved well matched and it was only in the second half that Cheshire secured a lead of 8 points to four.

Mr. Pickford, in opening-the—defer-Cce,-;;ICI the case divided itself into two ~,Lparts. First, did the plaintiff authorise the use of his name? and secondly, did he authorise the use of his name upon the soap now being sold, or were the defendants selling under his name a different and inferior soap? He (counsel) grieved to do it, but he had to ask the jury to say that the account of the matter given by Dr. Stuart was absolutely untrue, as shown by his own d,eolaration and the deelarations of other people which were used by him in his application for registration as the prescriber of the soap. There was no shadow of doubt that plaintiff knew that Crosfield's intended to sell the snap. If \lr. iViazzini Stuatt's evidence was cor- rect, there must have been absolute, downright, solid lies. Although all connected vvith Dr. Stuart knew that his honour was deeply hurt,and that his life was a misery the extraordinary thing was that the way they went to •assist him was not to get his name off the soap, but to ask for payment for keeping it on. Proceeding, counsel argued that after the defendants had submitted an analysis of the soap, in 1893, it was found satisfactory, and the matter was dropped for five years. He asked the jury to say that plaintiff knew all along that the soap was being sold, and to disbelieve his brother's evidence that plaintiff did not know about the renewed application for royalty in 1898. The whole thing had been a ques- tion of royalty, and when that was pointed out in 1898 to plaintiff's solicitors,the statement was not challenged and the matter was dropped again till 1903. The second part of the case was not whether the soap now sold was an ideal soap, but whether it was a libel to couple plaintiff's name with it. Upon that he would call medical, chemical, and other evidence to show that the article was good and ,satisfactory, and that, with the exception of some infinitesimal changes in manufacture, was the same as that recommended by the plaintiff twenty years ago, and ablelutely the same as indicated in the analysis in 1893._ ____

POOR BUT HAPPY. A PEEP IN THE RAGGED SCHOOL ML PARR ENTERTAINS 403 CHILDREN

The rafters of the Ragged School resounded with the merry mirth of over 400 poor but happy children on Wednesday evening on the occasion of a treat pro- vided by Mr. J. Charlton Parr. Mr. and Mrs. Parr were present, and their hearts must have been cheered by the happiness which they saw around them as the result of their generosity. Seated on wooiden benches, and packed closely together, were some 400 youngsters dipping into paper bags and withdrawing slices of bread and butter and cakes of various descriptions. In front of them were long tables bearing tins and cups, into which a number of vol- untary workers were pouring hot tea. In many a Warrington home such fare would not be considered much of a treat, but the delight depicted on the faces of the young recipients who were assembled in the Ragged School, seemed to indicate that such treats did not come their way very often andlthat the best must be made of the present opportunity. The depression in trade has caused acute distress in certain quarters of the town and some of the poorest children are amongst the chief sufferers. Many of them repair to the Ragged School for warmth and shelter in the evening, and here they meet with kindly-natured and sympathetic teachers who endeavour to influence them for good and train them in the way they should go. That these efforts are appreciated is proved by the willingness with which many of the children as they grow older, lend a helping hand in the work of the Ragged School. Of the many workers who were to be seen attending to the wants of the little ones on Wednesday evening, the great majority were young people who a few years ago were themselves young scholars in the school. They have been found situations in the town and are endeavouring to rise above the undesirable conditions in which their early years were spent. Mr. Parr's guests on Wednesday evening were for the most part ill-clad. Here and there, a collar was to be seen but cuffs and collars are rare at the Ragged School. The majority wore clothing, not exactly ragged, but ill-fitting, suggesting that it had been made for someone else, and after serving its purpose, had been given away as a cast-off. It was refreshing to see that the children came with clean hands and faces. However poor they may be there is no excuse for dirt and the officers endeavour to inculcate habits of cleanliness. They have not yet been successful in inducing the Water Committee to give them a supply, of water, so that the children may have a good "tub- bing," but this will come, we imagine bye and bye At the close of the repast, Mr. Skelland, the veteran superintendent, blew a whistle and there was instant attention. It was evident that discipline is not a negligible quantity at the Ragged School. At the superintendent's suggestion ,hearty cheers were raised for Mr. and Mrs. Parr, and none who heard those cheers could deny that there is a great deal of latent lung power among the children of the poor. They afterwards adjourned to the room upstairs where ithey had further opportunities of developing it at a. I magic lantern entertainment. Photos of local cele- I brides were shown on the screen and they were cheered in a measure proportionate to the children's knowledge of them as patrons of the Ragged School. In looking round the premises one cannot fail 0 be struck with their adaptability for the purposes to which they have been put. An upper floor has been put in and there is now an excellent room for meet- i• ings upstairs. The lower portion is used for smaller '• meetings, and there is a servicable kitchen, and care_ takers living rooms in close proximity. What wa I formerly the Registrar's office is now used as a coma Imittee room, and immediately above there is a room- ' which would make an excellent reading room, but lack of funds has, up to the present, prevented it from being used for such a purpose. This I old County Court building has not yet I lost all its dinginess. The old gas brackets give I but a fitful glimmer, and the window frames are of I such an antiquated type as to require immediate atten- tion. The Committee are desirous of completely renovating the premises. Up to the present they have been concerned in paiiing for the building. The ;whole of the cost, upwards of ,Sl,OOO has been raised, chiefly through the instrumentality of the drum and fife band, and the committee are now in possession of a building, standing on freehold land, the entire cost of which has been paid. Efforts are now being put forth for renovating the building. The walls- are very dirty and require cleaning. Then the lighting needs attention, and a suggestion has been made to introduce electric light. The necessary alterations will involve .an expenditure of £2OO, in addition to the usual expenses of the school, and it is believed that the money will be hrthcoming if the work is undertaken in earnest. It is the desire of the committee to make the school a greater centre for religious and social purposes than hitherto. There is no reason why the Ragged School should not become the acknowledged institution of the town for giving a helping hand to poor children who cannot help themselves. J. H.

A Damns GRIEVANGE

ACTION AGAINST MESSRS JOSEPH CR3SFIELD AND SONS, LIMITED. Dr. Pater Stuart's Skin Soap. DAMAGES ONE FARTHING.

At the Liverpool Assizes on Slturday Mr. Justice Jell and a special jury began the hearing of an action in which the plaintiff is Dr. Peter Stuart, of Rodney- street, Liverpool, and the defendants are Messrs. Joseph Crosffeld and Sons, Limited, soap manufac- turers, Warrington. The action was brought,according to the pleadings, to prevent the defendants from selling a soap manufactured by them as the plaintiff's soap and as recommended by the plaintiff, and also from issuing' a circular which the plaintiff said was libellous. The de- fence was that the plaintiff had allowed the defendants so to manufacture and sell the soap for a series of years without any attempt to prevent them. It was further denied that the circular is libellous. Mr. Taylor, ICC., and Mr.M'Conkey were counsel for the.plaintiff,and Mr. Pickford, K.C., and Mr. Steuart Brown for the defendants. _ _ _ _ _ _

Taylor said that though the plaintiff claimed damages it was not that he wanted to make any money out of the matter, but he sought to obtain an injunc- tion to stop allibel by the issue of certain pamphlets or circulars. In the medical profession one. of the rules obServed was that a practitioner mast not allow him- self to be advertised, so that his professional brethren might say that he was seeking in that way to increase his practice as against those with whom he was in com- petition. Such conduct would be regarded as disgrace- ful. For some years the defendants had been manufac- turing and selling in boxes a soap which was described as " Dr. Peter Stuart's celebrated skin soap, prepared by Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Warrington." It was stated on the boxes that the soap was guaranteed pure and neutral, eminently suitable for delicate and senti- tive skins, and soothing, refreshing, and invigorating. In 1902 a leaflet was inserted in the boxes, headed "Dr. Peter Stuart's skin soap," and stating that the soap was specially prepared from the prescription of a well-known skin specialist. Counsel said he should endeavour to show that in fact the soap was one of the least beneficial soaps for delicate and sensitive skins.lt appeared that in 1882 William Welsby, who was in the employ of Joseph Crosfleld and Sons (the defendant company's predecessor), was a patient of the plaintiff's, and after a conversation with the doctor Welsby said he believed he could produce a good skin soap. Some' time afterwards hd brought some samples to the plaintiff, and the defendants alleged that a verbal arrangement was come to between the doctor and Virelsby whereby the doctor agreed to recommend the soan to his patient 3. From 1831 until 1896 the plaintiff did not know what the defendants were doing with the soap in a commercial sense, but , defendants said that they had been making it up to the present time. In 1893 the use of the plaintiff's name in connection with the soap was brought to the notice of the plain- tiff's brother, who thereupon wrote to the defendants without the plaintiff's authority, suggesting that they should pay the plaintiff a royalty. There was also a letter written to the def)ndants by the plaintiff's wife, but so far as the plaintiff was concerned there was no demand for royalty and he did not receive a penny, but he complained to the defendants of the loss of reputa- tion and the nuisance of being applied to for supplies of the soap. An analysis of the soap recently showed that it contained an undue amount of • caustic soda, which irritated the skin. Whatever might have been the original transaction, plaintiff maintained that the soap now being sold by.defeniants as Dr. Peter Stuart's skin soap was certainly not a soap which he prescribed, recommended, and guaranteed. The plaintiff in evidence, denied that he arranged with the man Welsby about the use of his (Dr. Stuart's) name in cpnneetion with the soap. As a result of the use of his name and the soap being sold at retail shops his life became a burden, because people were constantly thinking it was his soap. Amongst the medical pro- fession it was also a matter of common adverse com- ment.

Mr. Pickford pointed out that the circulars were not used after March, 1903. _

The plaintiff gave further evidence as to having the soap analysed, and said that the soap was quite contrary to his ideas.

Cross-examined by Mr. Pickford, on Monday, plaintiff said he was positive he did not ask Welsby, defendants' foreman, in 1882 to make the soap. Counsel pointed out that in a declaration when applying to get his name registered for this trade mark, plaintiff had said that he suggested to Welsby that his firm should make a soap which would be a medical soap in the proper sense of the term. The plaintiff said he should never have thought of suggesting such a thing without it being put to him first. Pressed, however, he said," I probably must have suggested it." Mr. Pickford said the declara- tion was made in March, 1903, and plaintiff gave his reason for suggesting that Crosfield's should make a soap. Asked if he gave Welsby a receipt, plaintiff said he practically told him what the soap was to contain. Witness understood that he was to be supplied with as much of the soap as he required, but he did not think Crosfield's were, going to sell the soap. Plaintiff denied having referred people to Crosfield's in order that they might get the soap, but his attention was called to a declaration of a tradesman named Shaw, in which it was stated that plaintiff in 1893 told Shaw that Cros- field's were the original makers, and that they were the people to applyto for it. Witness said he did not remember the circumstances, but if it was in the declaration it must be true. He did not know that the soap was being sold with his name on it. He never gave permission, and never suspected that they would go on making it, because it was made specially for plaintiff, and him only. Mr. Pickford again called attention to a declaration in which plaintiff said, "IA a few years—four orsix— after the commencement of the manufacture the sale ceased as far as I know." Asked if he did not know whether the sale was going on until this time plaintiff replied that he did not know. By his Lordship: He did not know that the sale began tolhis knowledge, and he did not understand the word" cease " in the declaration.

Lordship:His Are you going to repudiate what you have sworn as a conscientious declaration under an Act of Parliament ?—No, my lord. _ .

His Lordship: You belong to an educated profes- sion:and are not like a person who does not understand, By Mr. Pickford : When he stated in a declaration that he believed that Messrs. Crosfleld had ceased mak- ing the soap, plaintiff said he referred to the original soap. Witness specially objected to his name on a soap which he had not prescribed, and he also objected to his name on the soap he recommended because it was to be made for him alone, and because if it bacarne public he ran the risk of losing his degrees. When ha referred Shaw to Crosfleld's he thought Shaw would be told the soap had died out. He made application for registration in connection with certain brands of soap, not for trade purposes, but to prevent the use of his name by other people. He denied having in 1898 in- structed his brother to ask Messrs. Crosfleldfor a royalty, and said that the declaration of Welsby that he (plain- tiff) gave permission for the name to be used was false. He did not see a letter from Mr. John Crosfield stating "that the name Dr. Peter Stuart was adoped by the firm many years ago, and that the long period, some 20 years, for which they had, with Dr. Stuart's knowledge, used the name, entitled them to do so."

Mazzini Stuart, brother of the plaintiff, deposed that in 1893 plaintiff asked witness to get his name off the soap, and he applied a,t)3olutely on his own authority to Gros&lds fora royalty. By Mr. Pickford :—Witness admitted that when he wrote to Crosflelds in 1898 to the effect that he was acting under his brother's instructions, it was "a busi- ness lie." (Laughter). His Lordship said he was sorry to hear the laughter, for he could never understand why a lie should be told in business lady more than in anything else. In reply to Mr. Pickford, witness afterwards said that he thought his brother told him in 1898 to get rid of plaintiff's name off the soap. Plaintiff left the matter entirely in witness's hands, and he thought by obtaining a royalty or acknowledgment it would be a step towards getting his brother's name off the soap. William White Murray, soap manufacturer, gave evi- dence as to the sale of" Dr. Peter Stuart's soap " dur- ing the time he was traveller for Messrs. Crosfield. His Lordship said that if the soap was bad soap, it was, of course, objectionable to have the plaintiff's name coupled with it; but if the soap was not bad and was substantially what it was originally when sanctioned he should think there would be no libel.

Ernest Richard Royston, patent agent, Liverpool,' deposed to the plaintiff consulting him with a view to putting a stop to the soap. Witness explained the steps he took in regard to the registration. Alfred Smeatham, analytical chemist, spoke as to analysing a piece of "Dr. Peter Stuart's Soap" in March, 1903. It was most decidedly not what he would call a skin soap, and was undoubtedly not suitable for delicate and sensitive skins. .

_ Dr. Stopford Taylor. skin specialist, of Liverpool, said he would not call the soap in question a skin soap; it would have an injurious effect on the skin, and in regard to young children would have a scalding effect on them. If an article was advertised under the name of a medical man his professional brethren looked down on him, and if he continued to have his name adver- tisei the medical council would strike his name off the register as having bean guilty of infamous conduct in a , Professional respect. Apart from the medical council it would do a medical man great harm to have his name advertised in connection witha soap which was a bad one. Other evidence of a similar character was given by Dr. F. H. Barendt, Dr. Thos. Stevenson, Dr. Edward Mahoney, Dr. Watson, and Dr. W. Hepworth. Mrs. Stuart, wife of the plaintiff, said that, with regard to a letter she wrote to the defendants' patent agent in 1898, with reference to royalties on the soap. she wrote that when her husband was in America, and when he returned she did not mention it to him. By Mr. Pickford : She never wanted a royalty. The witnesses *ailed for the plaintiff on Tuesday were people who had used the soap. Gertrude Lane, a refined looking young woman, slid

DR. MARKEL CROSS-EXAMINED.

Karl Emile Markel,chemist, and a managing director of the defendant firm, deposed that he used the "Dr Peter Stuart's soap" in 1886 and 1897, and the present soap was practically the same. He could not swear to any knowledge of the composition of the soap until 1893. Since that year no alteration had been made ex- cept the small changes necessary to meet the difference in the quality of the other materials used. The soap was not injurious, and no complaints of injury had been received. The firm did not allow any toilet soap- to go out that showed the slightest trace of free caustic sodi. T'aey could not carry on their business if they applied the test of Mr. Smetham, becauseno soap could be made without a small quantity of carbonate of soda, which was very different from caustic soda. The amount of caustic, if any, develoßed by this soap in washing was infinitesimal;' whether it was caustic or not was debateable; and if it was caustic it was developed more in other soaps. Cross-examined: Witness said that cocoanut oil re- quired more caustic soda than any other oil, but the more was put in the more difficult it was to dissociate it when treated with water.

Hive you ever manufactured Pears' soap? Witness (to his lordship) : Is that a fair question, my lord ? _

Well, I will put it another way. Have you ever manufactured soap without cocoanut oil in it ?—We have never made a toilet soap yet without cocoanut oil in it. Mr. Taylor proposed ;to read the various items in a " charge " for a" boil" of soap, sod began—" Eight tons of Australian mutton, second quality " Witness objected that it was scarcely fair to reveal trade secrets.

Have you ever manufactured Vinolia soap ?—No. Is it a good toilet soap ?—Yes, but not so good as Dr. Peter Stuart's celebrated soap. (Loud laughter.) Dr. Joseph Adams (Warrington) testified to the value of the defendants' soap. He had prescribed it for two patients, James Alfred Sutton and Miss Eliza Hodges both suffarers from eczerna,whom it had done great good. The patients were called and corroborated.

Charles Frederick Haffam, a director of-ti Ce. defendant company, said that before the five years ended Decem- ber, 1902, at least 400,000 tablets of Dr. Peter Stuart's soap had been sold; probably more. Owing to the plaintiff's complaint they had destroyed 3,000 of the leaflets and were prepared to stop the issue of more. Cross-examined: Witness said the sales of Dr. Stuart's soap for the last six years averaged about two tons every four months; they were less before that date. Other evidence was to the effect that the soap had been sold in Liverpool since 1884, and that it began to be boomed in 1893 ; also that it had been used for shaving without complaint. One witness said his baby had not been washed with any other soap. (Laughter.) Mr. Taylor: A good commercial baby, I should say. (Laughter.) A buyer in defendants' employ stated that the best. or Cochin, cocoanut oil was used in this soap, and that was some pounds per ton dearer than ordinary cocoanut oil, palm oil, or tallow. Dr. Markel (recalled) said that the ordinary and not the "peerless ' Erasmic Soap had the same base as the "Peter Stuart Soap," and cheap material was not used in its manufacture. _ _ _ _

ThiS-COlWZilihe evidence for the defence. The Verdict—One Farthing Damages. On Wednesday Mr. Pickford, K.C., in his address to the jury for the defence, said that his clients would not have been materially affected if they had taken the plaintiff's name off the soap, but it would be a hardship to the retailers, who had spent time, trouble, and money in establishing a business in this soap. If Dr. Stuart had said that this was an indiscretion of his earlier professional life, it was quite conceivable that the defendants would have taken a favourable view of his request. They had not been approached in that way, but, on the contrary, he said in effect that they had stolen his name, and that the soap was deleterious ; and no self-respecting firm could have acceded to a demand launched in that way. Mr. Taylor, K. C. in his reply, complained of the use made of Mrs. Stuart's letter as unfair, and as hitting below the belt, in order to keep up dividends from plaintiff's name Iwithout 'any conside ration, and said that the least said about the honour of the firm the better. There was one great blot on the foundation of the case. Welsby was not an agent of the defendants, but a foreman boiler, and he asked the jury to say that the loose conversation which plaintiff had with him in 1883 was not a binding business transaction. No record was taken of the matter, there was no pecuniary con- , sideration ; and yet the case was that plaintiff had sold the use of his name and had even given Welsby a dis- cretion to alter the character of the soap at any time las he liked. Whatever might be the jury's view of Mr. Mazzini Stuart's letters to the defendants asking for a royalty, the net result was that plaintiff objected to the use of his name, so that there was no assent by conduct. The present soap was not the soap assented to by the plaintiff, and there was no evidence to the contrary, although there must have been plenty of people who knew the composition of the original article. Counsel contended that it had been made out that his was not a skin soap; that it was not made from the plaintiff's prescription or description; that no competent medical man could recommend it for delicate and sensitive skins; and that the published description of the soap was defamatory of the plaintiff. His Lordship, in summing up, stated that in the face of the evidence it was difficult to believe that the plaintiff did not authorise the public use of his name in connection with the soap in 1884. As to the question of libel, the jury would probably find whether what was complained of was libellous or not according to whether they believed that the present soap was deleterious or not; and they would also have to say whether the libel was true or not. They might think that there was libel, and that the merest nominal damages would cover it, but if they thought the plaintiff's reputation had been seriously attacked without any fault of his, it would them become a question of substantial damages. The jury answered the eight questions put to them by the judge as follows :- 1. Did the plaintiff authorise the public use of his name in connection with the soap manufactured in 1884 ?—Yes. 2. Is the soap now being sold substantially the same as that manufactured in 1884 ?—Yes. 3. Is the soap now being sold so deleterious to the skin that no competent skin specialist would recom- mend it ?—No. 4. Are the words printed on the soap and boxes a libel on the plaintiff ?—No. 5. Are they true ?—Yes. 6. Was the leaflet (which alleged that the soap was made from plaintiff's "prescription ") a libel on plain- tiff ? Yes.

7. Was it true ?—No. 8. If either or both are libels on plaintiff what damages ?-0 ne farthing. Judgment was reserved.

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN,

LALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour, and Social Problems."

===== 0002601_19040227_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
LAND AND LABOUR PROBLEMS IN LIVERPOOL.

"The common growth of Mother Earth Suffices me—her tears, her mirth— Her humblest mirth and tears."

-WORDSWORTH.

As I rambled through the busy streets of Liverpool the other day, dodging its electric cars at every turn, and running the risk of getting killed at every crossing, I had visions of the Liverpool of long ago, I saw it in its infancy, when it was but a series of marshes and mud puddles, inhabited only by seagulls and wild birds. Then I saw it in its childhood, when it was a fishing village, with a few whitewashed, thatched houses, and a frowning castle,that was the house of its proud manorial lord. Then it grew into a town with docks,and quays,and warehouses, and the story of its growth is one of the romances of civilisation. vil nif I.—YOU CAN NEVER MAKE LAND VALUABLE TILL YOU CAN PERSUADE LABOUR TO SETTLE UPON IT. •

Six or seven centuries ago both sides of the Mersey were huge rabbit warrens, and swamps, and forests. A few straggling huts and rude cottages were to be met with occasionally, but the men who lived there spent their time in hunting and fishing, and the land for miles round was uncultivated. It was outside the pale of civilisation, and nobody would buy it at any price. The poverty of Liverpool in the Middle Ages was proverbial. You meet with evidence of it on every page of its history. In 1229 Henry 111 " granted a charter, and made it a free borough and guild merchant," and for all this honour and glory, and pomp and state, he naturally expected some revenue from its crown lands. But he only got a beggarly ten pounds a year for ail the land in the place. Will anyone tell me what the rlhtal of that same land is to-day? You can multiply that ten pounds by ten thousands of pounds and then you will notexpress the land values of Liverpool. What made these wild wastes and lonely marshes and sandhills so valuable? Human labour and enterprise. If millions of able-bodied men and women had not conspired to build Liverpool, it would have been a rabbit warren ind a valley of desolation to this day. I ' n 1283 all the! land at Woodside only produced a rental of forty shillings a year. Those rents were hardly worth the risk and trouble of collecting, for the Cheshire shore in those days was reckoned part of "Wild Wales," and it was a safe hiding place for those outlaws who had outrun the constable. Any landlord who risked his life in those swamps and forests, squeez- ing rents out of unwilling tenants, would richly deserve the forty shillings when he got them. Plague and pestilence, and famine and civil war, had much to answer for in those stormy times; And yet in the piping times of peace the average workman had not much to complain about. He might be a Christian on less than a pound a week. Houses were cheap in those days, and rents were low. During the reign of Queen Elizabeth a good .house in Castle-street was sold for ten pounds, and another was let for four shillings a year, or a trifle under a penny a week. What glorious times those were for working men. Wheat was only a shilling a bushel, and the aver- age labourer earned fourpence a day! So thht by working only one day a week he could earn a peck of wheat and a week's rent of house. Where is the man that could do that now? _

I know some workmen who have to work half the week to pay rents and rates, and taxes, and on Thursday morningl they begin to work for their own bread and cheese. In those far-off times working men did not get much money,but they got a good deal of money's worth, in the shape of material comforts. When they did get a bit of money they stowed it away in some hiding place, and lay low and said nothing. In 1626 Charles I. imposed his illegal tax of ship- money, and his tax collectors squeezed out of the na- tion every penny they could. They bled the good peo- ple of Bristol to the tune of a thousand pounds and they managed to get a hundred pounds out of the citizens of Chester, but with all their squeezing they only got a paltry twenty five pounds out of "poor Liverpool." The Liverpool people of those days were either very poor, or they knew how to stick to a bit of money when they got it.. i In 1700 there was another census in Liverpool taken, showing a population of only 5,714 souls. There were sixty small ships belonging to the port,but their united tonnage was only 4,000 tons. The whole fleet put together could not carry as much as one of our modern sailing ships, and the rateable value of all the houses, lands, and docks in the town was only .223,440. If you capitalise this at thirty years' purchase, you will find the whole show was worth only £703,000 two centuries ago. Now I think I have produced evidence enough to prove that as long as Liverpool was small, and had hardly any inhabitants in it, the land values were but trifling, and kings and manorial lords tried in vain to bring prosperity to the place. The fact is, towns and cities, like men and women, rise and fall, or grow and decay, in obedience to certain well-known laws of nature that we cannot control. If you dry up the supply of labour,and divorce men from the soil,you will bring stagnation and death to the fairest spot on earth. If you turn into any given spot a stream of human energy that must spend itself to keep itself alive you will always increase the land values of that particular spot, and you will work some mighty social revolu- tions.

IL-LIVERPOOL POURED ALL ITS ENERGIES INTO THE SHIPPING TRADE.

Two hundred years ago there was a boom. in the slave trade. Our West Indian planters were hard up for cheap labour for their sugar and coffee and cotton plantations, and they were willing to pay a big price for able-bodied niggers and ask no questions. In 1709 the men of Liverpool fitted up a small thirty-ton sloop and sent her with a well- armed crew to the West Coast of Africa on a slave- raiding expedition. She captured heaps of niggers,and sold them as slaves, and fairly coined money for her owners This traffic in human flesh and blood caught on, and in less than fifty years there were more than sixty ships earning this blood money. When George 111. was king we were at war with all the world, and we had not a navy equal to the task we set ourselves, so we granted kletters of marque to any bold buccaneer or cut-throat pirate who cared to ask us. We covered the seas with these licensed pirates, and Liverpool grew enormously rich by these ventures. Privateering and slave-hunting are not exactly orthodox Christian employments, but they are quite as honest as land-grabbing, and nearly all the fortunes made in Liverpool in those days came through 'these three channels. When ships were multiplied and fortunes were made by a few lucky voyages, new docks, and wharves, and quays, and warehouses had to be built to accommodate the growing trade. All these things required land, and then the wily land grabbers had their innings. The people of Liverpool never took kindly to these social parasites, and some rich stories are told of the rows and ructions that took place about this eternal land question. The monks of Whalley begged some land on the Cheshire side, and built a priory at Birken- head. They improved the shining hour by setting up a ferry boat of their own and building waiting rooms on both sides df the river. But the men of Liverpool would not have the bread taken out of their mouths after this fashion, so they rose in their wrath and demolished the shelter-houses, and mobbed the ferry- men and cut their boats adrift, and played the very mischief with all and sundry. In 1672 Lord Molyneaux, the ancestor of Lord Sefton, bought a piece of Crown land near Parliament-fields for i £450. It s worth to-day over £lOO,OOO a year. When he had got his new possession he began to build a fine house on the common land outside his own domain to assert his manorial rights. But the men of Liverpool raised a serious row about that new house. They chivied his workmen off, and pulled it down to its foun- dations, and scattered his building materials to the four winds. Lord Molyneaux was glad to make his peace with them by leasing a thousand acres of sand hills that were not his for a thousand years to the Cor- poration for thirty pounds a year. These sand hills are now the most valuable parts of the city, and they yield a rental of over half a million a year, that goes to the relief of the rates instead of into Lord Sefton's pocket There was a resettlement of this lease in 1700, and in 1777 the reversion of the lease was conveyed absolutely. to the Corporation.

Lord Derby '..riser in his generation than Lord Sefton. He did not attempt to steal the land on which Liverpool stands. He bought it for an old song, and refused to sell it, except under compulsion, and then only at famine prices. In 1710 he bought the whole of Moore's estate, which consisted of thousands of acres of freehold land in thirteen towns hips, but chiefly in Bank-hall, Bootle, Kirkdale, and Liverpool, for £12.000 The price seems ridiculously low now, but I have no doubt it was as much as it was worth at the time. The new era of commercial prosperity, and the big boom in trade, set in just after Lord Derby had bought Moore's estate, and, as he had cornered all the available land, he could sell it at his own price or hold it for a rise. He sold to the township of Kirk dale a strip of river frontage, about a thousand yards in length, for £17,500. He also sold 270,000 square yards of land for the first dock at Bootle for £90,000, and nearly the whole of that land was reclaimed from the sea, at the expense of the Bootle ratepayers.

. -- By the time you have mastered these facts you will believe me when I say that Moore's estate to-day is worth as many pounds as it was worth farthings when Lord Derby bought it. lII.—LAND VALUES HAVE RISEN IN CONSE- QUENCE OF THIS INDUSTRIAL REVOLUTION. Every new ship that was built in the port, increased the demand for dock and quay and warehouse accom-

Warrington.—Printed and Published by JOHN WALKER. & CO., at The Examiner General Printing Works, Bewp3y-street, and published by them at the same place,and also at The Examiner Offices,Victoria-road, WIDNES; 32, George-street, ST. HELENS; 3, Market- street, EARLESTOWN; Church-street, RUNCORN, FEB. 27TH 1904.

===== 0002601_19040227_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
modation, and these could not be provided till the claims of the landowner had been satisfied.. Every new workman who brought his labour into the town had to be housed and fed somewhere, so. cottages and workmen's dwellings had to be built by thousands, and the landowner reaped a nice harvest out of these. Every new road, and street, and highway and railway swallowed up more land and poured more goldfinto the:land grabbers' pocket. In 1700 the population .of the whole town was only 5,714, all told. In 1901 it was 684,947, so Liverpool has about one hundred and twenty times as many people in it as it had two centuries ago as the result of this great industrial revival.

Take another test. In 1700 its rateable value was £23,440. Its ratable value is now over four millions. So that it is worth about one hundred and seventy times as much now as it was two centuries ago, and its rate- able value is rapidly and steadily increasing, because the same causes are still at work.

I have shown you how Liverpool attracted to itself a mighty stream of human energy that built its docks, .and wharves, and warehouses, and transformed its sand- hills into streets and highways. This stream of energy naturally overflowed, and worked miracles at Birkenhead ! and Bootle, and all the towns in the immediate neigh- bourhood.

The rateable value of the borough of Birkenhead is now £508,500. If you capitalise this at thirty years' purchase you have property worth .£15255,000 to-day instead of the forty shilling freehold in the days of King Edward I.

80-otle in 1861 had only a population of 6,500, but in 1891. it had 53,544 residents, and its rateable value last year was £477,397. If you capitalise this at thirty years' purchase you find that the community has created property worth £14,321,910, instead of the barren, lonely sandhills of half a century ago. The rateable value of the three boroughs is more than five millions,. and this represents a capital value of £150,000,000. This vast unearned increment has been created by the labour, and the wisdom, and the energy of the com- munity, and the rich land-owners, who have pocketed the plunder, have not contributed one penny out of their rents to the improvement of their own property. I have given you this interesting object lesson because I want to show you where the working man comes in, and how he fares. He finds the labour in return for his wages, but he also contributes five or six shillings in the pound on his rental to thelratessthat are spent in making these great improvements. The city is made beautiful largely at his expense, and then his rents and rates go up because- his surroundings have been improved by this outlay. The only man who comes off well in this deal is the ground landlord, who simply hoards his land, as a miser hoards his gold. It costs him nothing to keep his barren building land, because we do not make him pay rates and taxes for it. So we have a few millionaires who have grown rich beyond the dreams of avarice out of public improvements of this kind. They are hoard- ing land till it has reached famine prices, while millions of theirneighbours have not land enough to dig their own graves. Some day we shall have wit enough to tax all land values—whether land is in use or not—and we shall compel the land-owner to sell it for public improvements at something like the price at which it is assessed. We shall not submit to legalised robbery and blackmailing for ever. But it takes John Bull a long time to cut his wisdom teeth, or to get a new idea into his head. When he thinks for himself, and finds how sweetly he is being swindled every, day, there Will be ructions. And when he begins to kick over the traces and raise a row, may I be there to see.

SEARCHING FOR A CANDIDATE. Warrington Labour Party's Disappointment. _— -- Mr. Alexander Wilkie, of Newcastle-on-Tyne will not contest Warrington in the labour interest at the, next general election. The fact was made known at a meeting of the Labonr Representation Committee, held in Bold-street schools on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mr. T. Plumb. Mr. 0. Buckley (secre- tary) read the following letter from the Executive Committee of the Associated Shipwrights' Society : Dear Sir, • Your letters re Warrington, and the report or representatives, have been repeatedly befoce our committees. Therefore, viewing the whole ques- tion, our committees regret they are unable to advise our general secretary, Mr. A. Wilkie, in his present position, to contest Warrington.— Yours faithfully, THOS. SWAN, Executive Chair- man. A letter was also read from Mr. Henderson, M.P., regretting the decision of Mr. Wilkie's executive. He can, he states, well understand the disapppintment of the Warrington Committee, after waiting so long, and promises to do his utmost to enable them to find another candidate. The meeting passed the following resolution : Having heard the reply from the Executive. Committee of the Shipwrights' Society, re Mr. Wilkie's candidature for Warrington, this com- mittee instructs the secretary to apply to the headquarters of the Labour Representation Com- mittee for the provision of a suitable labour can- didate for this borough.

HARRIERS.

WARRINGTON ATHLETIC CLUB. In miserable weather on Saturday last tho Warring ton Athletic Club along with twenty eight other clubs was competing in the Junior Cross Country Champion- ship at Haydock Park. In this race C. J. Straw once- more provedhis abilities as a long distance runner by beating a field of 250 runners and winning easily, By being the second local man to finish James Hall proved himself no mean performer in this his first champion- ship race his position being fifteenth. On counting up thepoints it was found that Warrington were just beaten for third place. Sutton being first, Hallam- shire and Wibsey Park, (two Yorkshire Clubs) being second:and third Warrington being in the same position as last year (fourth).

BOROUGH .(JDITOR.—TO THE ELECTO RS OF THE COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTO N. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— At the request of several ratepayers I have consented to come forward as a candidate for the position of one of your Auditors, and in doing so I may say I am not the nominee of any club or party. lam a Memner of the Institute of Chartered Account- ants in England and Wales, and as such I come before you from a professional point of view. This is my native town; therefore I am no stranger. and I think well known to many of you. Trusting for your support on the election day, on Tuesday, the Ist of March, at the Gymnasium, I have the honour to remain Ladies and Gentlemen, Yours very faithfully. JOSEPH RIGBY WILLIA.MS. 40, Sankey street, Warriagton, 22nd February, 1904.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WAFNINGTON.— EDUCATION COM VITTEE. ADMISSION EXAMINATION FOR -PUPIL TEACHERS. The Board of Education will hold an EXAMINATION on SATURDAY. MAY 14TH, 1904, to enable candidates to qualify for engagements as pupil teachers. The examination Is open for boys and girls who will be sixteen years of age on or before August Ist, 1904. Pupil teachers are paid, Boys £25 in the first year and £3O in the second, and Girls £l5 and £2O respectively. ADMISSION EXAMINATION FOR PREPANATORY PUPIL TEACHERS. The Board of Education will hold an examination on Sat- urday, May 14th, 1904, to enable candidades to qualify for engagements as Preparatory Pupil Teachers. The examina- tion is open to boys and girls who will be afteen years of age on or before August Ist, 1904. Th 9 successful candi- dates will attend the preparatory Pupil Teachers' Classes for one year. They will not be required to pay fees nor for their books, and will receive a scholarship of £5, Both examinations are open to Boys and Girls from Private Secondary, or Elementary schools. There are several vacancies in the schools in the Borough, and intending candidates can obtain on or before Friday April Bth. the syllabus of examination and further informa- tion from the undersigned. J. MOORE MURRAY, M.Sc. Secretary and Director of Education.

INSTANT RELIEF, RAPID CURE OF GOUT AND RHEUMATISM. BY EADE'S PILLS. A RECORD CURB ! 32, Archer-street, Bayswater, London, W. Dear Sir,—Sanday night my big toe pained me, on Monday it was much worse, and in the evening I could scarcely walk. At 6 p.m. I sent for a bottle of your Gout Pills, and took two at once. At 9.30 I was much relieved, and could eat my supper. I took another before going to bed, and although so hot I got a fair night's rest. I have taken two more pills to-day, and now, at 6.30, lam alright again. This is a record cure. Yours truly. PHILIP BRANS. EADE'S GOUT PILLS Are perfectly safe in their action, eliminate all injurious matter, and are highly restorative. STOCKTON HEATH. -- - [Continued from page 3.] IMPROVEMENTS IN THE VILL A.GE.—The proposed erection of two shops with cocoa rooms with club and billiard rooms at the corner of Old London-road and Victoria Square has • naturally aroused considerable interest in Stockton Heath. We have made further inquiries in the matter and we find that the proposed buildings will be somewhat unique in character as it is intended that there will be an arcade to each of the two shops. The buildings will be somewhat pretty design and undoubtedly owing to their close proximity to the proposed tramway terminus in Victoria-square the Cocoa Rooms should prove to ba quite a boon to visitors. The owner we understand is Mr. Peers- Egerton-Warburton, and the contractor Mr. C. W. Davenport.
